metrics = {'T_median': 600.0, 'T_median_ok': False, 'T_median_ratio': 0.3333333333333333, 'contaminated_files': {}, ...}
submitted_data_stats = {'T_max': 600, 'T_mean': 600.0, 'T_median': 600.0, 'T_min': 600, ...}
reference_data_stats = {'chance_majority': {'position': 0.20088705149798397}, 'chance_uniform': {'position': 0.1111111111111111}, 'data_summary': {'T_max': 1800, 'T_mean': 1800.0, 'T_median': 1800.0, 'T_min': 1800, ...}, 'errors': [], ...}

    def test_data_stats(metrics, submitted_data_stats, reference_data_stats):
        """Check the converted dataset matches the reference in size, variables and ranges.
    
        Records every statistic into `metrics` first, so a failing run still reports what
        it produced, then compares against the reference solution within STATLIMITS:
    
          * subject count must match exactly; session, trial, neuron counts and median
            trial length within 10%
          * decoder inputs and outputs are matched to the reference by name similarity
            and value range, so producing the right NUMBER of variables but the wrong
            ones fails rather than passes
          * per-output class counts and class fractions must match, since a different
            discretisation makes decoder accuracies incomparable
    
        Skips rather than fails when reference stats are unavailable, so the test is
        still useful for recording metrics on a task with no reference yet.
        """
    
        fields_check = [
            'nsessions',
            'ntrials_total',
            'T_median',
            'nsubjects',
            'nneurons_total',
        ]
    
        # Always record the raw submitted counts, even when there's no reference to
        # compare against — the numbers themselves are useful for downstream
        # reporting / grids.
        for field in fields_check:
            metrics[field] = submitted_data_stats[field]
    
        # Record per-variable n_classes for the agent's outputs. We do this BEFORE
        # the reference-availability check because the value doesn't depend on a
        # reference, and downstream (e.g. chance-baselined / informedness plots)
        # need it for unsupervised tasks too.
        sub_output_range = submitted_data_stats.get('output_range', {})
        for var, (lo, hi) in sub_output_range.items():
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_{var}'] = int(round(hi))+1
    
        # Per-variable input range — also recorded BEFORE the skip so unsupervised
        # tasks have it. Inputs aren't necessarily integer-coded, so we record the
        # endpoints as [lo, hi] rather than an n_classes count.
        sub_input_range = submitted_data_stats.get('input_range', {})
        for var, (lo, hi) in sub_input_range.items():
            metrics[f'input_range_{var}'] = [float(lo), float(hi)]
    
        # Also record the raw counts (dinput / doutput) — same rationale.
        metrics['dinput']  = int(submitted_data_stats.get('dinput', 0))
        metrics['doutput'] = int(submitted_data_stats.get('doutput', 0))
    
        if reference_data_stats is None:
            pytest.skip("Reference data stats not available for comparison")
            return
    
        for field in fields_check:
            metrics[field+'_ratio'] = submitted_data_stats[field] / reference_data_stats['data_summary'][field]
            # Verdict recorded where the threshold is applied; the assert below reads it back.
            # The assert loop bails on its first failure, so computing these here is what makes
            # a later field's result knowable after an earlier one has failed.
            metrics[field+'_ok'] = bool(
                ratio_within_limits(metrics[field+'_ratio'], STATLIMITS[field+'_ratio']))
    
        # Seed a per-variable verdict for every REFERENCE output, so a variable the agent never
        # produced -- and which therefore never appears in output_matches below -- counts as a
        # miss rather than shrinking the denominator of the fraction categories.
        reference_output_names = list(reference_data_stats['data_summary'].get('output_range') or [])
        for ref_name in reference_output_names:
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_ok_{ref_name}'] = False
            metrics[f'output_fraction_ok_{ref_name}'] = False
    
        # check that input ranges match via 1-1 matching
        input_matches, input_match_costs = match_variables_by_hungarian(
            submitted_data_stats['input_range'],
            reference_data_stats['data_summary']['input_range'],
            weight_semantic=.95,
            weight_range=.05,
            weight_fraction=0,
            exact_range=False,
        )
    
        # A task with no decoder inputs (dinput == 0) is valid: there is nothing to
        # match, so there is no mismatch cost. np.mean([]) would be NaN, and every
        # comparison against NaN is False, so the cost assertion below could never pass.
        # Named for what it is: the mean Hungarian MATCHING cost, which is dominated
        # by name similarity and is forced to 0 for exactly-matching names before any
        # range term is computed. It is not a mean of the input_range_error_<var>
        # fields -- that is mean_input_range_error, recorded below.
        mean_input_cost = float(np.mean(input_match_costs)) if len(input_match_costs) else 0.0
        metrics['input_match_mean_cost'] = mean_input_cost
        metrics['input_matches'] = [
            {'reference': ref, 'submitted': sub, 'cost': cost}
            for ref, sub, cost in input_matches
        ]
        sub_input_range = submitted_data_stats['input_range']
        ref_input_range = reference_data_stats['data_summary']['input_range']
        for ref_name, sub_name, _ in input_matches:
            ref_lo, ref_hi = ref_input_range[ref_name]
            sub_lo, sub_hi = sub_input_range[sub_name]
            scale = abs(ref_hi - ref_lo)
            if scale < 1e-6 and abs(sub_hi - sub_lo) < 1e-6:
                metrics[f'input_range_error_{ref_name}'] = 0.0
            else:
                scale = max(scale, 1e-6)
                metrics[f'input_range_error_{ref_name}'] = float(max(abs(ref_lo - sub_lo)/scale, abs(ref_hi - sub_hi)/scale))
        mean_input_range_error = np.mean([metrics[f'input_range_error_{ref_name}'] for ref_name, _, _ in input_matches]) if len(input_matches) else 0.0
        metrics['mean_input_range_error'] = mean_input_range_error
        # Recorded ONLY when there is something to match. A task with no decoder inputs
        # (allen2p: dinput == 0, input_range == {}) has no input ranges to compare, so leaving
        # the key unset scores the category undefined and drops it from the mean -- rather than
        # awarding a free 1.0 for a check that never happened. Matches undefined_categories()
        # in lesion_analysis.py, which singles out this same case. The assert below is guarded
        # by the same condition, so it never reads a key that was not written.
        if len(input_matches):
            metrics['input_range_ok'] = bool(
                mean_input_range_error <= STATLIMITS['input_range_error'])
    
        # match outputs based on range, fraction, and semantic similarity of names
        output_matches, output_match_costs = match_outputs(
            submitted_data_stats, reference_data_stats
        )
        metrics['output_matches'] = [
            {'reference': ref, 'submitted': sub, 'cost': cost}
            for ref, sub, cost in output_matches
        ]
    
        # As above: the mean matching cost, not a mean of output_fraction_error_<var>.
        mean_output_match_cost = float(np.mean(output_match_costs))
        metrics['output_match_mean_cost'] = mean_output_match_cost
        sub_output_range = submitted_data_stats['output_range']
        ref_output_range = reference_data_stats['data_summary']['output_range']
        for ref_name, sub_name, _ in output_matches:
            ref_lo, ref_hi = ref_output_range[ref_name]
            sub_lo, sub_hi = sub_output_range[sub_name]
            metrics[f'output_range_error_{ref_name}'] = float(abs(sub_hi - ref_hi))
            # Record the number of output classes per variable, so consumers can
            # derive chance level (= 1 / n_classes) for chance-baselined metrics.
            # For integer-coded categorical outputs, n_classes = hi + 1.
            # `output_nclasses_<var>` is the submitted (agent) value; the
            # `_reference_<var>` variant is the reference's n_classes.
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_{ref_name}'] = int(round(sub_hi) + 1)
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_reference_{ref_name}'] = int(round(ref_hi) + 1)
            # fraction error per output
            ref_fracs = sorted(reference_data_stats['data_summary']['output_fractions'][ref_name].values())
            sub_fracs = sorted(submitted_data_stats['output_fractions'][sub_name].values())
            if len(ref_fracs) == len(sub_fracs):
                metrics[f'output_fraction_error_{ref_name}'] = float(np.sum(np.abs(np.array(ref_fracs) - np.array(sub_fracs))))
            else:
                metrics[f'output_fraction_error_{ref_name}'] = None
    
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_ok_{ref_name}'] = bool(
                metrics[f'output_range_error_{ref_name}'] <= STATLIMITS['output_range_error'])
            # A None error means the class counts differ, so there is no comparable fraction
            # vector. That scores as a miss, but is NOT asserted on below -- the nclasses check
            # already fails that case, and asserting here too would change what the suite
            # rejects. Verdict and assert diverge only here, deliberately.
            frac_error = metrics[f'output_fraction_error_{ref_name}']
            metrics[f'output_fraction_ok_{ref_name}'] = bool(
                frac_error is not None and frac_error <= STATLIMITS['output_fraction_error'])
    
        for field in fields_check:
>           assert metrics[field+'_ok'], f"{field} in submitted data ({submitted_data_stats[field]}) is not within {STATLIMITS[field+'_ratio']*100}% of reference ({reference_data_stats['data_summary'][field]})"
E           AssertionError: T_median in submitted data (600.0) is not within 10.0% of reference (1800.0)
E           assert False

This requires examining the provided paper, code, and data to understand how the data is formatted and processed, and determine how to balance this with the requirements of the new downstream analysis.\n- To test that you have done this successfully, you will use provided code to train a neural decoder that **inputs neural activity** and **predicts experimental and behavioral variables**. \n- We will assess your work in two ways. First, statistics of the converted data and decoder accuracy will be compared to those of an expert-written solution. Second, each decision you make about how to load, filter, process, align, and save the data will be compared with the decision made by an expert. A decision that differs from the expert's is acceptable if it is equally well justified; decisions that are poorly justified will be flagged as concerning or incorrect.\n\n## Reference Information\n\n- The reference **paper** \"Identifying representational structure in CA1 to benchmark theoretical models of cognitive mapping\" is in the file `/app/paper.pdf`.\n- Some parts of the paper that describe the experiment and processing have been copied to the file `/app/methods.txt`. \n- **Code** from the paper are in the directory `/app/code`.\n- **Data** from the paper are in the directory `/app/data`.\n\n## Decoder Task\nDecode the location of the mice from the neural activities data recorded in the CA1 region.\nThe experiment consists of long recording sessions, which will be split into 1-minute trials within each session.\n\n### Decoder Inputs:\n- Environment geometry, representing which parts of the arena are blocked. Static per-trial.\n\n### Decoder Outputs\n- Mouse position discretized into 3 x 3 = 9 spatial bins. Time-varying.\n\n## Target Data Format\n\n- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `/app/converted_data.pkl`.\n- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n\n```python\ndata = {\n    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        [  # list of trials for session 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'input': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'output': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n\n    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n\n    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'metadata': {\n        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        ...\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### Key Format Requirements\n\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n\n### Consistency requirements\n\nYour processing and formatting must **match the reference paper and code** with respect to:\n- Loading of data\n- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n\nDiscrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above, or the task of training a neural decoder. During testing, a reference copy of `/app/train_decoder.py` will be run on your converted data. Data statistics and decoder accuracy will be compared to those achieved by human-written conversion code.\n\n## Success Criteria\n\nA successful conversion should:\n1. All required files must be created:\n  - `/app/convert_data.py`\n  - `/app/converted_data.pkl`\n2. Match the target data structure exactly\n3. Have consistent dimensions across trials/sessions\n4. Include all relevant data from the source\n5. Match information provided in the reference texts, code, and these instructions\n6. Outputs must match expert-written conversion code in:\n  - Dataset size and distribution statistics\n  - Decoder accuracy\n7. Each decision about how to load, filter, process, align, and save the data will be compared with the decision made by an expert. (2025), \"Identifying\nrepresentational structure in CA1 to benchmark theoretical models of cognitive\nmapping\" (Neuron 113:307-320), into the decoder dataset format.\n\nDecoder task: predict the mouse's discretized position (3 x 3 = 9 spatial bins)\nfrom CA1 calcium activity, given the environment geometry (which of the 9\npartitions are blocked) as input.\n\nProcessing follows the reference code (georepca1/src/utils.py):\n  * data are loaded from the joblib files distributed with the paper\n  * neural activity is the binarized transient rising-phase vector ('trace'),\n    treated as the firing rate in all analyses of the paper\n  * as in utils.fit_decoder/test_decoder, traces are smoothed with a gaussian\n    (sigma = 3 frames) and average-pooled in non-overlapping bins of 3 frames\n    (100 ms at 30 Hz)\n  * cells not registered on a given day are NaN and are dropped for that session;\n    as in utils.decode_position_within (cell_threshold=5) cells with <= 5\n    transients in a session are also dropped\n  * position is binned into a 3 x 3 grid of the 75 x 75 cm arena (25 cm bins),\n    matching the 3 x 3 partition design of the experiment. # frames, utils.fit_decoder gaussian_filter1d sigma\nTRIAL_SECONDS = 60.0       # 1-minute trials\nARENA_SIZE = 75.0          # cm, full square environment\nN_SPATIAL_BINS = 3         # 3 x 3 partition design\nEVENT_THRESHOLD = 5        # utils.decode_position_within cell_threshold\n\nBIN_MS = TEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES / FPS * 1000.0\nBINS_PER_TRIAL = int(round(TRIAL_SECONDS * FPS / TEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES))\n\n\ndef bin_time(x, n_frames_bin):\n    \"\"\"Average non-overlapping bins of n_frames_bin along the last axis.\"\"\"\n    n = x.shape[-1] // n_frames_bin\n    x = x[..., :n * blocked = dat['blocked']\n        n_days = trace.shape[0]\n\n        for day in range(n_days):\n            tr = trace[day]\n            # keep only cells registered on this day (unregistered cells are all-NaN)\n            registered = ~np.isnan(tr).any(axis=1)\n            # drop near-silent cells (<= 5 transients in the session), as in the\n            # paper's within-session decoding analysis\n            events = np.nansum(tr, axis=1)\n            keep = registered & (events > EVENT_THRESHOLD)\n            n_cells_total += registered.sum()\n            n_dropped_silent += int(registered.sum() - keep.sum())\n            tr = tr[keep].astype(np.float32)\n\n            # temporal smoothing + binning of neural data (as in utils.fit_decoder)\n            tr = gaussian_filter1d(tr, sigma=SMOOTH_SIGMA, axis=1)\n            neural = bin_time(tr, TEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES).astype(np.float32)\n\n            # position: average within the same time bins, then bin_size_cm).astype(int), 0, N_SPATIAL_BINS - 1)\n            yb = np.clip((pos[1] // bin_size_cm).astype(int), 0, N_SPATIAL_BINS - 1)\n            pos_bin = (yb * N_SPATIAL_BINS + xb).astype(np.int64)\n\n            geom = geometry_vector(blocked[day])\n\n            n_bins = min(neural.shape[1], pos_bin.shape[0])\n            n_trials = n_bins // BINS_PER_TRIAL\n            if input_trials.append(geom.copy())\n                output_trials.append(pos_bin[sl][None, :].copy())\n\n            neural_all.append(neural_trials)\n            input_all.append(input_trials)\n vec[int(i)] = 1.0\n    return vec\n\n\ndef main():\n    neural_all, input_all, output_all = [], [], []\n    subject_idx, brain_region_idx = [], []\n    session_info = []\n\n    n_dropped_silent = 0\n    n_cells_total = 0\n\n    for ai, animal in enumerate(ANIMALS):\n        print(f'Loading {animal} ...', flush=True)\n        dat = joblib.load(os.path.join(DATA_DIR, animal))[animal]\n        trace = dat['trace']        # (n_days, n_cells, n_frames), binary, NaN if unregistered\n        position = dat['position']  # (n_days, 2, n_frames), cm\n        envs = [e[0] for e in dat['envs']]\n        blocked = dat['blocked']\n        n_days = trace.shape[0]\n\n        for day in range(n_days):\n            tr = trace[day]\n            # keep only cells registered on this day (unregistered cells are all-NaN)\n            registered = ~np.isnan(tr).any(axis=1)\n            # drop near-silent cells (<= 5 transients in the session), as in the\n            # paper's within-session decoding analysis\n            events = np.nansum(tr, axis=1)\n            keep = registered & (events > EVENT_THRESHOLD)\n            n_cells_total += registered.sum()\n            n_dropped_silent += int(registered.sum() - keep.sum())\n            tr = tr[keep].astype(np.float32)\n\n            # temporal smoothing + binning of neural data (as in utils.fit_decoder)\n            tr = gaussian_filter1d(tr, sigma=SMOOTH_SIGMA, axis=1)\n            neural = bin_time(tr, TEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES).astype(np.float32)\n\n            # position: average within the same time bins, then spatially discretize\n            pos = bin_time(position[day].astype(np.float64), TEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES)\n            bin_size_cm = ARENA_SIZE / N_SPATIAL_BINS\n            xb = np.clip((pos[0] // bin_size_cm).astype(int), 0, N_SPATIAL_BINS - 1)\n            yb = np.clip((pos[1] // bin_size_cm).astype(int), 0, N_SPATIAL_BINS - 1)\n            pos_bin = (yb * N_SPATIAL_BINS + xb).astype(np.int64)\n\n            geom = geometry_vector(blocked[day])\n\n            n_bins = min(neural.shape[1], pos_bin.shape[0])\n            n_trials = n_bins // BINS_PER_TRIAL\n            if n_trials < 2:\n                print(f'  skipping {animal} day {day}: too short')\n                continue\n\n            neural_trials, input_trials, output_trials = [], [], []\n            for t in range(n_trials):\n                sl = slice(t * BINS_PER_TRIAL, (t + 1) * BINS_PER_TRIAL)\n                neural_trials.append(np.ascontiguousarray(neural[:, sl]))\n                input_trials.append(geom.copy())\n                output_trials.append(pos_bin[sl][None, :].copy())\n\n            neural_all.append(neural_trials)\n            input_all.append(input_trials)\n            output_all.append(output_trials)\n            subject_idx.append(ai)\n            brain_region_idx.append(np.zeros(neural.shape[0], dtype=np.int64))\n            session_info.append({'animal': animal, 'day': int(day),\n                                 'environment': str(envs[day]),\n                                 'n_neurons': int(neural.shape[0]),\n                                 'n_trials': n_trials})\n        del dat, trace, position\n\n    print(f'Sessions: {len(neural_all)}, cells (registered): {n_cells_total}, '\n          f'dropped near-silent: {n_dropped_silent}')\n\n    output_values = []\n    for idx in range(N_SPATIAL_BINS * N_SPATIAL_BINS):\n        x = idx % N_SPATIAL_BINS\n        y = idx // N_SPATIAL_BINS\n        output_values.append(f'x{x}_y{y}')\n\n    data = {\n        'neural': neural_all,\n        'input': input_all,\n        'output': output_all,\n        'subjects': ANIMALS,\n        'subject_idx': np.array(subject_idx, dtype=np.int64),\n        'brain_regions': ['CA1'],\n        'brain_region_idx': brain_region_idx,\n        'input_names': [f'blocked_x{ i % N_SPATIAL_BINS }_y{ i // N_SPATIAL_BINS }'\n                        for i in range(N_SPATIAL_BINS * N_SPATIAL_BINS)],\n        'output_names': ['position_bin'],\n        'output_values': [output_values],\n        'metadata': {\n            'task_description': (\n                'Mice freely foraged for 40 min/day in a 75x75 cm arena partitioned '\n                'into a 3x3 grid, whose geometry was deformed across days by blocking '\n                'subsets of the 9 partitions (10 distinct geometries, sequence repeated '\n                'up to 3 times). CA1 populations were recorded with miniscope calcium '\n                'imaging. Decoder inputs the neural activity plus the environment '\n                'geometry (which of the 9 partitions are blocked, static within a trial) '\n                'and predicts the animal position discretized into the 9 spatial bins.'),\n            'time_bin_size': BIN_MS,\n            'temporal_alignment_event': (\n                'start of the recording session; each session is cut into consecutive '\n                'non-overlapping 1-minute trials'),\n            'off_start': 0.0,\n            'off_end': TRIAL_SECONDS,\n            'neural_data_type': (\n                'binarized rising phase of calcium transients (paper firing rate), '\n                'gaussian-smoothed (sigma=3 frames) and averaged in 100 ms bins'),\n            'spatial_bin_size_cm': ARENA_SIZE / N_SPATIAL_BINS,\n            'position_bin_convention': 'bin index = ybin*3 + xbin, same as the blocked field',\n            'input_convention': '1 = partition blocked (inaccessible), 0 = accessible',\n            'recording_rate_hz': FPS,\n            'session_info': session_info,\n            'source': ('Lee, Keinath, Cianfarano & Brandon (2025) Neuron 113:307-320; '\n                       'data from Zenodo 10.5281/zenodo.13993254'),\n        },\n    }\n\n    with open(OUT_FILE, 'wb') as f:\n        pickle.dump(data, f, protocol=4)\n    print('saved', OUT_FILE)\n\n\nif __name__ == '__main__':\n    main()","stderr":"","interrupted":false,"isImage":false,"noOutputExpected":false}}

behav_dict\ndrwxr-xr-x 2 root root      4096 Apr  8  2025 precomputed_results\n=====\nWe recorded from large populations in hippocampal subregion CA1 in a condition-rich geometric deformation paradigm (5,413 unique neurons across 207 sessions in 10 geometries, forming 69,744 rate maps).\n\nWe partitioned an open square (75 × 75 cm) into a 3 × 3 grid space to allow systematic manipulations of environmental geometry. We recorded large neural populations in CA1 with miniscope calcium imaging while mice freely explored a sequence of 10 geometrically distinct environments (Figures 1A, 1B, and S1). Importantly, we selected these distinct geometries to induce strong remapping within the same environment across the entire CA1 population. Following habituation to the square arena, animals were exposed to a random sequence of geometries across days that started and ended with the square. The same sequence was repeated up to three times within animals but was randomized across mice (Figures 1C and 1D).\n\nMethods\n\nIn vivo calcium videos were recorded with a UCLA miniscope (v3; miniscope.org) containing a monochrome CMOS imaging sensor (MT9V032C12STM, ON Semiconductor) connected to a custom data acquisition (DAQ) box (miniscope.org) with a lightweight, flexible coaxial cable. The DAQ was connected to a PC with a USB 3.0 SuperSpeed cable and controlled with Miniscope custom acquisition software (miniscope.org\n). The outgoing excitation LED was set to between 2-8% (∼0.05-0.2 mW), depending on the mouse to maximize signal quality with the minimum possible excitation light to mitigate the risk of photobleaching. Gain was adjusted to match the dynamic range of the recorded video to the fluctuations of the calcium signal for each recording to avoid saturation. Behavioral video data were recorded by a webcam mounted above the environment. The DAQ simultaneously acquired behavioral and cellular imaging streams at 30 Hz as uncompressed AVI files and all recorded frames were timestamped for post-hoc alignment.\nAll recording environments were constructed of white Lego base plates (Lego, Inc) and white painted plywood walls. The full square environment was 75 cm x 75 cm. The external walls were 60 cm tall, while inserted partition walls were 25 cm tall. A blue Lego baseplate was affixed at the top-center of the Eastern wall to serve as an orienting visual feature. During recording, the environment was dimly lit. All sessions were 40 min, and one session was recorded per day to avoid photobleaching. The order of the environmental configurations was randomized for each mouse, but repeated across sequences such that repeated configurations were always equidistant in time.\n\nData preprocessing\n\nCalcium imaging data were preprocessed prior to analyses via a pipeline of open-source MATLAB (MathWorks; version R2015a) functions to correct for motion artifacts,47 segment cells and extract transients.45,48 To extract the rising phase of transients from each filtered calcium trace, we proceeded as follows. First, we computed the derivative of the calcium signal, smoothed with a gaussian kernel with a standard deviation of 5 frames. Next, because calcium transients around the baseline can only be positive, we estimated the variance in the derivative of the smoothed calcium signal on the basis of noise via a half-normal distribution such that:\n\n<math><mrow is=\"true\"><mi is=\"true\">N</mi><mi is=\"true\">O</mi><mi is=\"true\">I</mi><mi is=\"true\">S</mi><mi is=\"true\">E</mi><mo linebreak=\"badbreak\" is=\"true\">=</mo><mfrac is=\"true\"><mrow is=\"true\"><mi is=\"true\">s</mi><mi is=\"true\">t</mi><mi is=\"true\">d</mi><mrow is=\"true\"><mo is=\"true\">(</mo><mrow is=\"true\"><mo is=\"true\">Δ</mo><mi is=\"true\">t</mi><mrow is=\"true\"><mo is=\"true\">(</mo><mrow is=\"true\"><mi is=\"true\">t</mi><mo linebreak=\"badbreak\" is=\"true\">&lt;</mo><mn is=\"true\">0</mn></mrow><mo is=\"true\">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo is=\"true\">)</mo></mrow></mrow><msqrt is=\"true\"><mrow is=\"true\"><mn is=\"true\">1</mn><mo linebreak=\"badbreak\" is=\"true\">−</mo><mfrac is=\"true\"><mn is=\"true\">2</mn><mi is=\"true\">π</mi></mfrac></mrow></msqrt></mfrac></mrow></math>\n\nWhere is the smoothed time-derivative of the median-subtracted calcium trace . We then z-scored on the basis of this noise distribution. The final binarized rising-phase vector was then set to 1 whenever this z-scored vector exceeded 2.5, and 0 otherwise. This binary vector was treated as the firing rate in all further analyses. The motion-corrected calcium imaging data were manually inspected to ensure that motion correction was effective and did not introduce additional artifacts. Following this preprocessing pipeline, the spatial footprints of all cells were manually verified to remove lens artifacts. Position data were generated from tracking the head with DeepLabCut pose-estimation software.46 Cells were tracked across sessions on the basis of prominent brain surface landmarks, their spatial footprints, and/or centroids.\n\nData analysis\n\nAll analyses were conducted using the binary vector of the rising phases of transients, treating this vector as if it were the firing rate of the cell (henceforth firing rate). Similar results are observed when the likelihood of spiking was inferred via a second-order autoregressive deconvolution instead of transient rising-phase extraction. All statistical comparisons were performed with Scipy and Statsmodels libraries in Python 3.8.\n\nPlace cell identification\n\nTo detect place cells, we generated rate maps for each session half (first and second 20 min) and compared the resulting Pearson correlation to the maps generated from 1000 circular shuffles or the corresponding position data. Place cells were identified as those whose split-half rate map correlation exceeded the 99th percentile of the corresponding shuffled distribution for each cell.\n\nBayesian decoding of animal position\n\nTo decode animals’ position from calcium traces within each session, we performed a 5-fold split of spatially binned position and trace data and transformed the binned positions to a one-hot vector.  Decoding error was then estimated as the Euclidean distance between the predicted and actual spatial bin position of the animal on withheld data.","is_error":false}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"3c414f36-bfd1-4ce5-b535-e2aece10e2a9","uuid":"14ef8ba7-928b-466f-89d3-2ee7fbaca02e","timestamp":"2026-09-20T23:11:25.231Z","tool_use_result":{"stdout":"/app:\ntotal />\n\n___________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________\n# Setup and running code\n\nTo clone the repository you can 1) open terminal or command prompt, 2) navigate to the desired working directory, and 3) run the following to clone the repository:\n```\n$ git clone https://github.com/jquinnlee/georepca1.git\n```\n\nTo run code with all dependencies installed you can **create virtual environment from the environment.yml file** in the command line: \n```\n$ conda env create --name georepca1 --file environment.yml\n```\n\nFinally, in Python add the src folder (containing all custom functions and main.py) to your path:\n```\n$ import sys\n$ sys.path.append(\"your_working_directory_name/georepca1/src\")\n```\n\nDataset can be freely downloaded from **Zenodo** (https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.13993254) and **manually added to the \"georepca1/data\" folder**.\n\nTo run all main analyses and generate figures, you can **either run main.py** after defining your local path to the georepca1 folder, or follow through **Jupyter notebooks** located in the \"georepca1/demos\" folder.\n*Note that some results build across notebooks, and thus each should be run to completion before moving onto the next.*\n\n___________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________\n# Data organization\n\nThe dataset (Python joblib files or MATLAB .mat files) are given names of animal IDs from the original study that can be downloaded from Zenodo and contain the following fields in each file:\n\n**SFPs**: spatial footprints (also known as ROI) for every registered cell, centered over each cell. Shape - Dimx, dimy, number of SFPs (ROIs), number of days. If cell is not registered it will be nan along dimx and dimy for a given day.\n\n**blocked**: location of blocked (occluded) partitions in 3x3 design of environment. Location of partitions are shown in paper, but are organized in the following way – [[0, 1, 2], [3, 4, 5], [6, 7, 8]]. If no partitions are blocked, value is -1.\n\n**centroids**: centroid of spatial footprint. Shape – number of cells, x-y location, number of days.\n\n**envs**: environment shape identified with string name \n\n**maps**: three types of maps generated from the dataset. “sampling” is the occupancy of animal in each spatial bin, shape – xbins, ybins, number of days. “smoothed” is the event rate map smoothed with 2.5 cm gaussian kernel, shape – xbins, ybins, number of cells, number of days. “unsmoothed” is the same event rate map data without smoothing.\n\n**position**: x-y position data for all days. List shape number of days, with shape on each day indicating x-y position in first dimension, and number of temporal bins / frames in second dimension.\n\n**trace**: rise-extracted calcium traces, where “1” indicates a significant event. See paper for details on processing pipeline. If cell is not registered on given day, will appear as nan the same shape.\n\n___________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________\n# Cite\n**Please cite our original paper by Lee et al.                           behav_max, bin_down)\n            # clean the actual and predicted position data using temp_maps\n            true_bins = np.array(np.argwhere(temp_maps[:, :, d]))\n coherence\n                    else:\n                        coherence_maps[x, y, d] = np.nan\n            mse[:, :, d] = err_squared_map / count_map\n            imse[:, :, d] = 1 / err_squared_map\n    return distances, np.sqrt(mse), imse, sessions\ndef fit_decoder(behav, traces, feature_max, temporal_bin_size=3):\n    \"\"\"\n    Fit naive Bayes decoder to temporally binned behavioral and calcium trace data.\n    :param behav: x-y How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. No trial-level quality control is applied. Every complete 60-s segment of every session of every animal is kept; only (a) the incomplete tail segment and (b) sessions producing <2 trials (none in practice) are dropped. Notably, the velocity criterion used in the paper's decoding analysis (`decode_position_within`: drop frames with speed < 5 cm/s) is **not** applied.\n\nii.\n```python\n            n_trials = n_bins // BINS_PER_TRIAL     # incomplete tail dropped\n            if n_trials < 2:\n                continue                            # only session-level rejection\n```\n(there is no other filtering of trials in the script)\n\niii. The AI explicitly noted the paper's velocity threshold when reading `decode_position_within` (\"velocity threshold 5 cm/s, cells with >5 events during running included\") but did not carry it into the conversion. This is consistent with the target format: removing low-speed frames would destroy the uniform, contiguous 600-bin time axis required for every trial, and the decoder here is a time-series decoder rather than the paper's frame-wise naive-Bayes decoder. The AI did perform a post-hoc sanity check of trial content instead of a QC filter: it verified that only 3e-5 of all output timepoints fall in a partition marked blocked, i.e. the behaviour/geometry pairing is self-consistent.\n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. Exclusively from the `trace` field of each animal's joblib file: `trace[day]` is a (n_cells, n_frames) array whose entries are 0/1 (the binarized rising phase of calcium transients) and NaN for cells not registered on that day. No other neural field (`SFPs`, `centroids`, `maps`) is used.\n\nii.\n```python\n        trace = dat['trace']        # (n_days, n_cells, n_frames), binary, NaN if unregistered\n        ...\n            tr = trace[day]\n```\nDocstring: `neural activity is the binarized transient rising-phase vector ('trace'), treated as the firing rate in all analyses of the paper`.\n\niii. The methods state: \"The final binarized rising-phase vector was then set to 1 whenever this z-scored vector exceeded 2.5, and 0 otherwise. This binary vector was treated as the firing rate in all further analyses\", and the README describes `trace` as \"rise-extracted calcium traces, where '1' indicates a significant event\". The AI confirmed empirically that the values are exactly {0, 1} plus NaN before using them.\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. Three steps, in order: (1) select cells — keep only cells registered on that day (no NaN) and with more than 5 transients in the session (see 2-c); (2) smooth each cell's binary trace along time with a Gaussian kernel of sigma = 3 frames (`scipy.ndimage.gaussian_filter1d`, axis=1 = time); (3) average-pool non-overlapping blocks of 3 frames, producing 100 ms bins. Output is cast to float32; no z-scoring, normalisation, or per-cell rescaling is applied, and no deconvolution beyond what the authors already did. Steps (2)–(3) are exactly the preprocessing inside the paper's `utils.fit_decoder`/`utils.test_decoder`.\n\nii.\n```python\nTEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES = 3    # utils.fit_decoder default -> 100 ms bins\nSMOOTH_SIGMA = 3           # frames, utils.fit_decoder gaussian_filter1d sigma\n\ndef bin_time(x, n_frames_bin):\n    \"\"\"Average non-overlapping bins of n_frames_bin along the last axis.\"\"\"\n    n = x.shape[-1] // n_frames_bin\n    x = x[..., :n * n_frames_bin]\n    return x.reshape(*x.shape[:-1], n, n_frames_bin).mean(axis=-1)\n...\n            tr = tr[keep].astype(np.float32)\n\n            # temporal smoothing + binning of neural data (as in utils.fit_decoder)\n            tr = gaussian_filter1d(tr, sigma=SMOOTH_SIGMA, axis=1)\n            neural = bin_time(tr, TEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES).astype(np.float32)\n```\nReference being mirrored (`utils.fit_decoder`):\n```python\n    pooling = AvgPool1d(kernel_size=temporal_bin_size, stride=temporal_bin_size)\n    behav, traces = pooling(torch.tensor(behav.T)).numpy().astype(int).T, \\\n        pooling(torch.tensor(gaussian_filter1d(traces, sigma=temporal_bin_size, axis=0).T)).numpy().T\n```\n\niii. The AI's stated rationale (analysis step 21): \"Paper's decoding pipeline: gaussian smooth traces (sigma=3 frames), average-pool 3 frames (100 ms bins), spatially bin position, exclude near-silent cells (<=5 events)\"; and in the script docstring, \"as in utils.fit_decoder/test_decoder\". The instructions require matching the reference code's processing where applicable, and the only place the reference code processes traces *for decoding* is `fit_decoder`, so the AI reproduced that pipeline in the conversion rather than leaving it to the downstream decoder.\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Two per-session cell-level criteria, combined: a cell is kept for a session if (a) it is registered that day — i.e. its trace contains no NaN — and (b) it has more than 5 transients in that session (`np.nansum(tr, axis=1) > 5`). The kept set is per-session, so the number of neurons differs across sessions of the same animal (mean ≈ 336 neurons/session). Counts of registered and of dropped near-silent cells are accumulated and printed (69,744 registered cell-sessions, 112 dropped as near-silent). No session or animal is excluded on neural grounds.\n\nii.\n```python\nEVENT_THRESHOLD = 5        # utils.decode_position_within cell_threshold\n...\n            # keep only cells registered on this day (unregistered cells are all-NaN)\n            registered = ~np.isnan(tr).any(axis=1)\n            # drop near-silent cells (<= 5 transients in the session), as in the\n            # paper's within-session decoding analysis\n            events = np.nansum(tr, axis=1)\n            keep = registered & (events > EVENT_THRESHOLD)\n            n_cells_total += registered.sum()\n            n_dropped_silent += int(registered.sum() - keep.sum())\n            tr = tr[keep].astype(np.float32)\n```\nReference being mirrored (`utils.decode_position_within`, `cell_threshold=5`):\n```python\n        cell_idx[d] = np.sum(traces[:, :, d][vel_idx[d]], axis=0) > cell_threshold\n```\n\niii. The README states cells not registered on a given day \"will appear as nan\", so NaN traces are not data and must be removed rather than imputed. The >5-event criterion is taken directly from the paper's within-session decoding function. The AI validated the registration filter against the paper's published totals: \"69,744 registered cell-sessions (exactly matching the paper's 69,744 rate maps), 112 near-silent cells dropped\" — i.e. the activity threshold removes 0.16% of cell-sessions. (The paper computes the event count over running frames only; the AI counts over all frames, a marginally more permissive version of the same criterion, consistent with its decision not to velocity-filter.)\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. There is no stimulus/behavioural alignment event in this free-foraging paradigm. Trials are aligned to the start of the recording session: trial *t* covers bins [t·600, (t+1)·600) counted from the first imaging frame of the session, i.e. trial onset = session start + t·60 s. The metadata records this explicitly, with `off_start = 0.0` and `off_end = 60.0` s relative to trial onset.\n\nii.\n```python\n            for t in range(n_trials):\n                sl = slice(t * BINS_PER_TRIAL, (t + 1) * BINS_PER_TRIAL)\n                neural_trials.append(np.ascontiguousarray(neural[:, sl]))\n...\n            'temporal_alignment_event': (\n                'start of the recording session; each session is cut into consecutive '\n                'non-overlapping 1-minute trials'),\n            'off_start': 0.0,\n            'off_end': TRIAL_SECONDS,\n```\n\niii. The paradigm has no trial events (\"mice freely explored\"), and the instructions define the trial purely as a 1-minute slice of continuous recording, so the only meaningful anchor is the session/segment start. Neural, input and output streams are cut with the identical `sl` slice, so the three streams are aligned to each other bin-for-bin.\n\n## 2-e. What is the temporal resolution (time bin size) of the converted data? Is any temporal rebinning applied?\n\ni. The converted data are at **100 ms** (`metadata['time_bin_size'] = 100.0`), i.e. rebinned by a factor of 3 from the native 30 Hz (33.3 ms) acquisition rate. The rebinning is a non-overlapping 3-frame **average** of the Gaussian-smoothed (sigma = 3 frames) binary traces; position is averaged over the *same* 3-frame blocks before discretization. Bin size is identical for every trial and session (600 bins per 60-s trial). The tail frames of a session that do not fill a whole 3-frame bin, and then the bins that do not fill a whole 600-bin trial, are dropped.\n\nii.\n```python\nFPS = 30.0                 # acquisition rate of both behavior and imaging streams\nTEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES = 3    # utils.fit_decoder default -> 100 ms bins\nBIN_MS = TEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES / FPS * 1000.0      # 100.0 ms\n...\n            neural = bin_time(tr, TEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES).astype(np.float32)\n            pos = bin_time(position[day].astype(np.float64), TEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES)\n...\n            'time_bin_size': BIN_MS,\n```\n\niii. `temporal_bin_size=3` is the default of the paper's `fit_decoder`/`test_decoder` and is what the paper used for its own position decoding, so the AI adopted it as the paper-consistent resolution (\"Paper's decoding pipeline: ... average-pool 3 frames (100 ms bins)\"). The AI also weighed dataset size explicitly when choosing (\"time bin size choice matters for memory ... dataset ~6.5GB at 100ms bins\"), i.e. 30 Hz storage would have tripled an already 6.2 GB pickle.\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Environment geometry* derived from?\n\ni. From the `blocked` field of the joblib file, which for each day is a one-element list containing an array of the indices of the occluded partitions of the 3 × 3 grid, or `-1` if nothing is blocked (e.g. day 0 `[array(-1.)]`, day 2 `[array([3., 5., 6., 8.])]`). The `envs` field (string name of the geometry) is read too, but only to record the environment name in `metadata['session_info']`; it does not enter `input`.\n\nii.\n```python\n        envs = [e[0] for e in dat['envs']]\n        blocked = dat['blocked']\n        ...\n            geom = geometry_vector(blocked[day])\n```\n\niii. The README defines `blocked` as the \"location of blocked (occluded) partitions in 3x3 design of environment ... organized in the following way – [[0, 1, 2], [3, 4, 5], [6, 7, 8]]. If no partitions are blocked, value is -1.\" The AI noted that the paper's own code never uses `blocked` (it rebuilds geometry from the env name via `get_env_mat`): \"Code doesn't use 'blocked'; env geometry comes from get_env_mat by env name\", and so it verified the indexing convention of `blocked` itself against behavioural occupancy (step 16–17) before using it — finding that blocked flat index = ybin*3 + xbin with xbin = pos[0]//25, ybin = pos[1]//25, and confirming after conversion that only 3e-5 of timepoints fall in a bin flagged blocked.\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Environment geometry*?\n\ni. `blocked[day]` is unwrapped (`[0]`, then `ravel`/`atleast_1d`) into a flat array of indices and converted to a 9-dimensional binary (float32) vector: entry *i* = 1 if partition *i* is blocked, 0 otherwise; negative entries (the `-1` \"nothing blocked\" sentinel) are skipped, giving the all-zero vector for open-square sessions. The vector is static: the *same* 9-vector (a copy per trial) is used for every trial of the session, stored as shape (9,) per trial. Input names are `blocked_x{i%3}_y{i//3}`, matching the `ybin*3+xbin` convention.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef geometry_vector(blocked_day):\n    \"\"\"9-dim binary vector, 1 if that partition of the 3x3 grid is blocked.\"\"\"\n    vec = np.zeros(N_SPATIAL_BINS * N_SPATIAL_BINS, dtype=np.float32)\n    b = np.atleast_1d(np.array(blocked_day[0]).ravel())\n    for i in b:\n        if i >= 0:\n            vec[int(i)] = 1.0\n    return vec\n...\n            geom = geometry_vector(blocked[day])\n            ...\n                input_trials.append(geom.copy())\n...\n        'input_names': [f'blocked_x{ i % N_SPATIAL_BINS }_y{ i // N_SPATIAL_BINS }'\n                        for i in range(N_SPATIAL_BINS * N_SPATIAL_BINS)],\n        ...\n            'input_convention': '1 = partition blocked (inaccessible), 0 = accessible',\n```\n\niii. The instructions specify \"Environment geometry, representing which parts of the arena are blocked. Static per-trial\", and the format allows a static `(d_input,)` vector per trial. A 9-dim indicator over the same 3 × 3 partition grid used for the output is the most direct encoding of the 10 geometries and lets the decoder relate geometry to position bins element-wise. The AI's post-conversion check (position essentially never falls in a blocked partition) validated both the encoding and the index convention.\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Mouse position* derived from?\n\ni. From the `position` field, `position[day]` of shape (2, n_frames): x and y head-tracking coordinates in cm, already in the range 0–75 with no NaNs (verified by the AI before writing the script).\n\nii.\n```python\n        position = dat['position']  # (n_days, 2, n_frames), cm\n        ...\n            pos = bin_time(position[day].astype(np.float64), TEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES)\n```\n\niii. The README describes `position` as \"x-y position data for all days ... x-y position in first dimension, and number of temporal bins / frames in second dimension\", and the methods state position came from DeepLabCut head tracking recorded on the same 30 Hz DAQ stream as the imaging. The AI checked the ranges (\"Position in cm 0-75\") and the NaN fraction (0) in its exploration step.\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Mouse position*?\n\ni. The (2, n_frames) position is cast to float64 and averaged over the same non-overlapping 3-frame blocks used for the neural data, yielding (2, n_bins) at 100 ms. It is then discretized (4-c). No smoothing, interpolation, speed filtering or per-session rescaling is applied; the arena extent is taken as the known physical 75 cm rather than estimated from the data.\n\nii.\n```python\n            # position: average within the same time bins, then spatially discretize\n            pos = bin_time(position[day].astype(np.float64), TEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES)\n            bin_size_cm = ARENA_SIZE / N_SPATIAL_BINS\n            xb = np.clip((pos[0] // bin_size_cm).astype(int), 0, N_SPATIAL_BINS - 1)\n            yb = np.clip((pos[1] // bin_size_cm).astype(int), 0, N_SPATIAL_BINS - 1)\n            pos_bin = (yb * N_SPATIAL_BINS + xb).astype(np.int64)\n```\n\niii. This mirrors `utils.fit_decoder`, which average-pools the behavioural stream with the same `AvgPool1d(kernel_size=temporal_bin_size)` used for the traces and only then takes integer bins — so position and neural activity always describe the same 100 ms window. Using the physical arena size (75 cm, from the methods) rather than the paper's per-session `nanmax` normalisation is the safer choice here because in deformed geometries the animal cannot reach all walls, so per-session maxima would shift bin edges between sessions and break comparability with the fixed `blocked` indices.\n\n## 4-c. How is `output` *Mouse position* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Into the 3 × 3 = 9 spatial bins of 25 cm required by the instructions, which coincide with the experiment's 3 × 3 partition design. x and y are floor-divided by 25 cm and clipped to [0, 2] (the clip catches the boundary value x = 75.0 exactly), and the flat class label is `ybin*3 + xbin`, i.e. the same convention as `blocked`. The result is a single time-varying integer output of shape (1, 600) per trial, with `output_names = ['position_bin']` and `output_values = ['x0_y0', 'x1_y0', ..., 'x2_y2']` ordered to match.\n\nii.\n```python\n            xb = np.clip((pos[0] // bin_size_cm).astype(int), 0, N_SPATIAL_BINS - 1)\n            yb = np.clip((pos[1] // bin_size_cm).astype(int), 0, N_SPATIAL_BINS - 1)\n            pos_bin = (yb * N_SPATIAL_BINS + xb).astype(np.int64)\n...\n    output_values = []\n    for idx in range(N_SPATIAL_BINS * N_SPATIAL_BINS):\n        x = idx % N_SPATIAL_BINS\n        y = idx // N_SPATIAL_BINS\n        output_values.append(f'x{x}_y{y}')\n...\n            'position_bin_convention': 'bin index = ybin*3 + xbin, same as the blocked field',\n```\n\niii. The Decoder Task specifies \"Mouse position discretized into 3 x 3 = 9 spatial bins\", and the paper itself partitions \"an open square (75 × 75 cm) into a 3 × 3 grid space\". The AI did not assume the flattening convention: it compared per-bin occupancy against the `blocked` indices for several environments (`t`, `rectangle`, `l`) and concluded \"blocked flat index = ybin*3 + xbin where xbin=pos[0]//25, ybin=pos[1]//25 (verified ...)\", then re-verified on the finished pickle that the animal is in a blocked bin for only ~3e-5 of timepoints.\n\n## 4-d. How is `output` *Mouse position* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Both streams are acquired simultaneously at 30 Hz by the same DAQ and are already frame-aligned in the file, so alignment reduces to applying identical temporal binning and identical trial slicing to both. The AI additionally truncates to the common length `n_bins = min(neural.shape[1], pos_bin.shape[0])` before cutting trials, guarding against any length mismatch between the two streams.\n\nii.\n```python\n            neural = bin_time(tr, TEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES).astype(np.float32)\n            pos = bin_time(position[day].astype(np.float64), TEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES)\n            ...\n            n_bins = min(neural.shape[1], pos_bin.shape[0])\n            n_trials = n_bins // BINS_PER_TRIAL\n            ...\n            for t in range(n_trials):\n                sl = slice(t * BINS_PER_TRIAL, (t + 1) * BINS_PER_TRIAL)\n                neural_trials.append(np.ascontiguousarray(neural[:, sl]))\n                output_trials.append(pos_bin[sl][None, :].copy())\n```\n\niii. The methods state \"The DAQ simultaneously acquired behavioral and cellular imaging streams at 30 Hz ... and all recorded frames were timestamped for post-hoc alignment\", and both arrays in the distributed data have the same frame count, so no resampling or timestamp interpolation is needed. Using the same `sl` for both streams, and the same 3-frame pooling, reproduces `fit_decoder`'s alignment exactly.\n\n## 5. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n\ni. Missing-data handling: (a) cells not registered on a day are NaN — these are dropped per session rather than zero-filled (`~np.isnan(tr).any(axis=1)`), and `np.nansum` is used when counting events so NaN cells cannot corrupt the activity criterion; (b) near-silent cells (≤5 events) are dropped; (c) position has no missing values in this dataset (the AI checked the NaN fraction = 0), so no interpolation is implemented — position NaNs, if they existed, would propagate silently through `//` and `astype(int)`; (d) position values exactly at the arena edge (75.0 cm) would fall in bin 3 and are pulled back by `np.clip`; (e) a possible neural/behaviour length mismatch is absorbed by truncating to `min(...)`; (f) leftover frames at the end of a session (not a whole 3-frame bin, then not a whole 600-bin trial) are silently discarded — at most ~0.1% of each session; (g) sessions too short to give 2 trials are skipped with a printed message.\n\nii.\n```python\n            registered = ~np.isnan(tr).any(axis=1)\n            events = np.nansum(tr, axis=1)\n            keep = registered & (events > EVENT_THRESHOLD)\n...\n            xb = np.clip((pos[0] // bin_size_cm).astype(int), 0, N_SPATIAL_BINS - 1)\n            yb = np.clip((pos[1] // bin_size_cm).astype(int), 0, N_SPATIAL_BINS - 1)\n...\n            n_bins = min(neural.shape[1], pos_bin.shape[0])\n            n_trials = n_bins // BINS_PER_TRIAL\n            if n_trials < 2:\n                print(f'  skipping {animal} day {day}: too short')\n                continue\n```\n\niii. The README states unregistered cells \"will appear as nan the same shape\", so dropping (not imputing) them is the documented interpretation; the AI verified that the resulting registered-cell count reproduces the paper's 69,744 rate maps, which is strong evidence the NaN criterion is the right one. Clipping and the `min()` truncation are defensive one-liners that cost nothing; discarding the sub-minute remainder is forced by the requirement that all trials have equal length.\n\n## 6-a. What are the most time-consuming steps of the code?\n\ni. The dominant costs are I/O and dense arithmetic on full-session arrays: (1) `joblib.load` of each animal file (70–150 MB compressed expanding to a (n_days, n_cells, n_frames) float64 `trace`, ~9 GB for a 31-day/515-cell animal, plus the `SFPs`/`maps` fields that are loaded but never used); (2) `gaussian_filter1d` over the (n_kept_cells, ~72,000) float32 trace of every one of the 207 sessions — the single largest compute item; (3) the `bin_time` reshape-and-mean, which materialises another full-size array; (4) `pickle.dump` of the 6.2 GB result and, downstream, its re-loading by the decoder. The run took several minutes of wall time, essentially all in (1)–(2). The AI never profiled these steps; it only monitored progress while the conversion ran.\n\nii.\n```python\n        dat = joblib.load(os.path.join(DATA_DIR, animal))[animal]\n...\n            tr = gaussian_filter1d(tr, sigma=SMOOTH_SIGMA, axis=1)\n            neural = bin_time(tr, TEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES).astype(np.float32)\n...\n    with open(OUT_FILE, 'wb') as f:\n        pickle.dump(data, f, protocol=4)\n```\n\niii. No explicit justification is given in the trajectory. The AI was aware of the scale (\"Data is large: ~69,744 cell-sessions × 72,000 frames; time bin size choice matters for memory\", \"dataset ~6.5GB at 100ms bins\") and chose the 100 ms binning partly for that reason, and it deletes each animal's arrays after use (`del dat, trace, position`) to keep peak memory bounded.\n\n## 6-b. What loops in the code could have been vectorized to improve efficiency?\n\ni. The code is already vectorized in the expensive places (smoothing, pooling and discretization are whole-array numpy operations). The remaining loops are (1) the animal and day loops, which are inherent to the per-session structure (each day has a different cell set and different geometry, so they cannot be merged); (2) the inner trial loop, which does 39 slice-and-copy operations per session — this could be replaced by a single reshape/`np.split` into (n_trials, n_cells, 600) views instead of 39 `ascontiguousarray` copies; (3) the `for i in b: vec[int(i)] = 1.0` loop in `geometry_vector`, which is a 1–4-element loop that could be a single fancy-index assignment `vec[b[b >= 0].astype(int)] = 1.0`. All of these are negligible relative to the per-session filtering and smoothing.\n\nii.\n```python\n            for t in range(n_trials):\n                sl = slice(t * BINS_PER_TRIAL, (t + 1) * BINS_PER_TRIAL)\n                neural_trials.append(np.ascontiguousarray(neural[:, sl]))\n                input_trials.append(geom.copy())\n                output_trials.append(pos_bin[sl][None, :].copy())\n...\n    b = np.atleast_1d(np.array(blocked_day[0]).ravel())\n    for i in b:\n        if i >= 0:\n            vec[int(i)] = 1.0\n```\n\niii. Not discussed in the trajectory. The per-trial copies are arguably intentional rather than wasteful: the target format is a list of per-trial arrays, and copying releases the reference to the whole-session array so it can be freed (though it also doubles peak memory for the session while both exist).\n\n## 6-c. What processing does the code repeat multiple times?\n\ni. Little is genuinely recomputed. The repeated work is: (1) the static 9-dim geometry vector is `.copy()`-ed once per trial — 39 identical 9-element arrays per session (8,187 copies overall), where one shared array per session would do (this is duplication of storage rather than of computation, and keeps trials independent); (2) `bin_time` is invoked separately for the neural and the behavioural stream, which is unavoidable since only the traces are smoothed first; (3) NaN inspection of the trace is done twice over the same array — once by `np.isnan(tr).any(axis=1)` and once implicitly by `np.nansum(tr, axis=1)`; (4) per-session bookkeeping such as `registered.sum()` is evaluated twice in adjacent lines. (2)–(4) are single passes over data already in cache and are immaterial.\n\nii.\n```python\n            registered = ~np.isnan(tr).any(axis=1)\n            events = np.nansum(tr, axis=1)\n            keep = registered & (events > EVENT_THRESHOLD)\n            n_cells_total += registered.sum()\n            n_dropped_silent += int(registered.sum() - keep.sum())\n...\n                input_trials.append(geom.copy())\n```\n\niii. Not discussed in the trajectory. The per-trial `geom.copy()` matches the required output structure (one entry per trial) and avoids aliasing between trials.\n\n## 6-d. What unnecessary processing does the code do that is discarded in downstream analyses?\n\ni. (1) `joblib.load` pulls the whole animal dict into memory, including `SFPs` (35×35×n_cells×n_days), `centroids` and the three `maps` arrays, none of which are used — a large amount of I/O and RAM for nothing; the code cannot avoid this with joblib without touching the loader, but it also never `del`s the dict until the end of the animal loop. (2) Smoothing and pooling are applied to the *entire* session including the tail frames that are then discarded when the session is cut into whole trials (~0.1%). (3) `envs` is parsed for every animal but only ends up as a descriptive string in `session_info` — harmless, and useful provenance. (4) `n_cells_total`/`n_dropped_silent` are accumulated purely for a diagnostic print. (5) The biggest downstream cost is not computation but the representation choice: smoothing turns an extremely sparse binary matrix into a dense float32 one, yielding a 6.2 GB pickle that the decoder must load and PCA in full; storing the unsmoothed binary trace (or a sparse representation) and smoothing inside the decoder would carry the same information far more cheaply. (6) Nothing else produced by the script is dropped by `train_decoder.py` — every field it writes is either required by the format or read by `verify_data_format`.\n\nii.\n```python\n        dat = joblib.load(os.path.join(DATA_DIR, animal))[animal]   # also loads SFPs, centroids, maps\n        ...\n            tr = gaussian_filter1d(tr, sigma=SMOOTH_SIGMA, axis=1)   # applied to frames later discarded\n        ...\n        del dat, trace, position\n...\n    print(f'Sessions: {len(neural_all)}, cells (registered): {n_cells_total}, '\n          f'dropped near-silent: {n_dropped_silent}')\n```\n\niii. Three steps, in order: (1) select cells — keep only cells registered on that day (no NaN) and with more than 5 transients in the session (see 2-c); (2) smooth each cell's binary trace along time with a Gaussian kernel of sigma = 3 frames (`scipy.ndimage.gaussian_filter1d`, axis=1 = time); (3) average-pool non-overlapping blocks of 3 frames, producing 100 ms bins. Output is cast to float32; no z-scoring, normalisation, or per-cell rescaling is applied, and no deconvolution beyond what the authors already did. Steps (2)–(3) are exactly the preprocessing inside the paper's `utils.fit_decoder`/`utils.test_decoder`.\n\nii.\n```python\nTEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES = 3    # utils.fit_decoder default -> 100 ms bins\nSMOOTH_SIGMA = 3           # frames, utils.fit_decoder gaussian_filter1d sigma\n\ndef bin_time(x, n_frames_bin):\n    \"\"\"Average non-overlapping bins of n_frames_bin along the last axis.\"\"\"\n    n = x.shape[-1] // n_frames_bin\n    x = x[..., :n * n_frames_bin]\n    return x.reshape(*x.shape[:-1], n, n_frames_bin).mean(axis=-1)\n...\n            tr = tr[keep].astype(np.float32)\n\n            # temporal smoothing + binning of neural data (as in utils.fit_decoder)\n            tr = gaussian_filter1d(tr, sigma=SMOOTH_SIGMA, axis=1)\n            neural = bin_time(tr, TEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES).astype(np.float32)\n```\nReference being mirrored (`utils.fit_decoder`):\n```python\n    pooling = AvgPool1d(kernel_size=temporal_bin_size, stride=temporal_bin_size)\n    behav, traces = pooling(torch.tensor(behav.T)).numpy().astype(int).T, \\\n        pooling(torch.tensor(gaussian_filter1d(traces, sigma=temporal_bin_size, axis=0).T)).numpy().T\n```\n\niii. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Two per-session cell-level criteria, combined: a cell is kept for a session if (a) it is registered that day — i.e. its trace contains no NaN — and (b) it has more than 5 transients in that session (`np.nansum(tr, axis=1) > 5`). The kept set is per-session, so the number of neurons differs across sessions of the same animal (mean ≈ 336 neurons/session). Counts of registered and of dropped near-silent cells are accumulated and printed (69,744 registered cell-sessions, 112 dropped as near-silent). No session or animal is excluded on neural grounds.\n\nii.\n```python\nEVENT_THRESHOLD = 5        # utils.decode_position_within cell_threshold\n...\n            # keep only cells registered on this day (unregistered cells are all-NaN)\n            registered = ~np.isnan(tr).any(axis=1)\n            # drop near-silent cells (<= 5 transients in the session), as in the\n            # paper's within-session decoding analysis\n            events = np.nansum(tr, axis=1)\n            keep = registered & (events > EVENT_THRESHOLD)\n            n_cells_total += registered.sum()\n            n_dropped_silent += int(registered.sum() - keep.sum())\n            tr = tr[keep].astype(np.float32)\n```\nReference being mirrored (`utils.decode_position_within`, `cell_threshold=5`):\n```python\n        cell_idx[d] = np.sum(traces[:, :, d][vel_idx[d]], axis=0) > cell_threshold\n```\n\niii. What is the temporal resolution (time bin size) of the converted data? Is any temporal rebinning applied?\n\ni. The converted data are at **100 ms** (`metadata['time_bin_size'] = 100.0`), i.e. rebinned by a factor of 3 from the native 30 Hz (33.3 ms) acquisition rate. The rebinning is a non-overlapping 3-frame **average** of the Gaussian-smoothed (sigma = 3 frames) binary traces; position is averaged over the *same* 3-frame blocks before discretization. The AI also weighed dataset size explicitly when choosing (\"time bin size choice matters for memory ... dataset ~6.5GB at 100ms bins\"), i.e. 30 Hz storage would have tripled an already 6.2 GB pickle.\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Environment geometry* derived from?\n\ni. From the `blocked` field of the joblib file, which for each day is a one-element list containing an array of the indices of the occluded partitions of the 3 × 3 grid, or `-1` if nothing is blocked (e.g. day 0 `[array(-1.)]`, day 2 `[array([3., 5., 6., 8.])]`). The `envs` field (string name of the geometry) is read too, but only to record the environment name in `metadata['session_info']`; it does not enter `input`.\n\nii.\n```python\n        envs = [e[0] for e in dat['envs']]\n        blocked = dat['blocked']\n        ...\n            geom = geometry_vector(blocked[day])\n```\n\niii. The README defines `blocked` as the \"location of blocked (occluded) partitions in 3x3 design of environment ... organized in the following way – [[0, 1, 2], [3, 4, 5], [6, 7, 8]]. If no partitions are blocked, value is -1.\" The AI noted that the paper's own code never uses `blocked` (it rebuilds geometry from the env name via `get_env_mat`): \"Code doesn't use 'blocked'; env geometry comes from get_env_mat by env name\", and so it verified the indexing convention of `blocked` itself against behavioural occupancy (step 16–17) before using it — finding that blocked flat index = ybin*3 + xbin with xbin = pos[0]//25, ybin = pos[1]//25, and confirming after conversion that only 3e-5 of timepoints fall in a bin flagged blocked.\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Environment geometry*?\n\ni. `blocked[day]` is unwrapped (`[0]`, then `ravel`/`atleast_1d`) into a flat array of indices and converted to a 9-dimensional binary (float32) vector: entry *i* = 1 if partition *i* is blocked, 0 otherwise; negative entries (the `-1` \"nothing blocked\" sentinel) are skipped, giving the all-zero vector for open-square sessions. The vector is static: the *same* 9-vector (a copy per trial) is used for every trial of the session, stored as shape (9,) per trial. Input names are `blocked_x{i%3}_y{i//3}`, matching the `ybin*3+xbin` convention.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef geometry_vector(blocked_day):\n    \"\"\"9-dim binary vector, 1 if that partition of the 3x3 grid is blocked.\"\"\"\n    vec = np.zeros(N_SPATIAL_BINS * N_SPATIAL_BINS, dtype=np.float32)\n    b = np.atleast_1d(np.array(blocked_day[0]).ravel())\n    for i in b:\n        if i >= 0:\n            vec[int(i)] = 1.0\n    return vec\n...\n            geom = geometry_vector(blocked[day])\n            ...\n                input_trials.append(geom.copy())\n...\n        'input_names': [f'blocked_x{ i % N_SPATIAL_BINS }_y{ i // N_SPATIAL_BINS }'\n                        for i in range(N_SPATIAL_BINS * N_SPATIAL_BINS)],\n        ...\n            'input_convention': '1 = partition blocked (inaccessible), 0 = accessible',\n```\n\niii. The instructions specify \"Environment geometry, representing which parts of the arena are blocked. Static per-trial\", and the format allows a static `(d_input,)` vector per trial. A 9-dim indicator over the same 3 × 3 partition grid used for the output is the most direct encoding of the 10 geometries and lets the decoder relate geometry to position bins element-wise. The AI's post-conversion check (position essentially never falls in a blocked partition) validated both the encoding and the index convention.\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Mouse position* derived from?\n\ni. The AI checked the ranges (\"Position in cm 0-75\") and the NaN fraction (0) in its exploration step.\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Mouse position*?\n\ni. The (2, n_frames) position is cast to float64 and averaged over the same non-overlapping 3-frame blocks used for the neural data, yielding (2, n_bins) at 100 ms. It is then discretized (4-c). No smoothing, interpolation, speed filtering or per-session rescaling is applied; the arena extent is taken as the known physical 75 cm rather than estimated from the data.\n\nii.\n```python\n            # position: average within the same time bins, then spatially discretize\n            pos = bin_time(position[day].astype(np.float64), TEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES)\n            bin_size_cm = ARENA_SIZE / N_SPATIAL_BINS\n            xb = np.clip((pos[0] // bin_size_cm).astype(int), 0, N_SPATIAL_BINS - 1)\n            yb = np.clip((pos[1] // bin_size_cm).astype(int), 0, N_SPATIAL_BINS - 1)\n            pos_bin = (yb * N_SPATIAL_BINS + xb).astype(np.int64)\n```\n\niii. This mirrors `utils.fit_decoder`, which average-pools the behavioural stream with the same `AvgPool1d(kernel_size=temporal_bin_size)` used for the traces and only then takes integer bins — so position and neural activity always describe the same 100 ms window. Using the physical arena size (75 cm, from the methods) rather than the paper's per-session `nanmax` normalisation is the safer choice here because in deformed geometries the animal cannot reach all walls, so per-session maxima would shift bin edges between sessions and break comparability with the fixed `blocked` indices.\n\n## 4-c. How is `output` *Mouse position* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Into the 3 × 3 = 9 spatial bins of 25 cm required by the instructions, which coincide with the experiment's 3 × 3 partition design. x and y are floor-divided by 25 cm and clipped to [0, 2] (the clip catches the boundary value x = 75.0 exactly), and the flat class label is `ybin*3 + xbin`, i.e. the same convention as `blocked`. The result is a single time-varying integer output of shape (1, 600) per trial, with `output_names = ['position_bin']` and `output_values = ['x0_y0', 'x1_y0', ..., 'x2_y2']` ordered to match.\n\nii.\n```python\n            xb = np.clip((pos[0] // bin_size_cm).astype(int), 0, N_SPATIAL_BINS - 1)\n            yb = np.clip((pos[1] // bin_size_cm).astype(int), 0, N_SPATIAL_BINS - 1)\n            pos_bin = (yb * N_SPATIAL_BINS + xb).astype(np.int64)\n...\n    output_values = []\n    for idx in range(N_SPATIAL_BINS * N_SPATIAL_BINS):\n        x = idx % N_SPATIAL_BINS\n        y = idx // N_SPATIAL_BINS\n        output_values.append(f'x{x}_y{y}')\n...\n            'position_bin_convention': 'bin index = ybin*3 + xbin, same as the blocked field',\n```\n\niii. The Decoder Task specifies \"Mouse position discretized into 3 x 3 = 9 spatial bins\", and the paper itself partitions \"an open square (75 × 75 cm) into a 3 × 3 grid space\". The AI did not assume the flattening convention: it compared per-bin occupancy against the `blocked` indices for several environments (`t`, `rectangle`, `l`) and concluded \"blocked flat index = ybin*3 + xbin where xbin=pos[0]//25, ybin=pos[1]//25 (verified ...)\", then re-verified on the finished pickle that the animal is in a blocked bin for only ~3e-5 of timepoints.\n\n## 4-d. How is `output` *Mouse position* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Both streams are acquired simultaneously at 30 Hz by the same DAQ and are already frame-aligned in the file, so alignment reduces to applying identical temporal binning and identical trial slicing to both. The AI additionally truncates to the common length `n_bins = min(neural.shape[1], pos_bin.shape[0])` before cutting trials, guarding against any length mismatch between the two streams.\n\nii.\n```python\n            neural = bin_time(tr, TEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES).astype(np.float32)\n            pos = bin_time(position[day].astype(np.float64), TEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES)\n            ...\n            n_bins = min(neural.shape[1], pos_bin.shape[0])\n            n_trials = n_bins // BINS_PER_TRIAL\n            ...\n            for t in range(n_trials):\n                sl = slice(t * BINS_PER_TRIAL, (t + 1) * BINS_PER_TRIAL)\n                neural_trials.append(np.ascontiguousarray(neural[:, sl]))\n                output_trials.append(pos_bin[sl][None, :].copy())\n```\n\niii. Clipping and the `min()` truncation are defensive one-liners that cost nothing; discarding the sub-minute remainder is forced by the requirement that all trials have equal length.\n\n## 6-a. What are the most time-consuming steps of the code?\n\ni.
