What variables in the raw data is `input` *Environment geometry* derived from?\n\ni. From the `blocked` field of the joblib file, which for each day is a one-element list containing an array of the indices of the occluded partitions of the 3 × 3 grid, or `-1` if nothing is blocked (e.g. day 0 `[array(-1.)]`, day 2 `[array([3., 5., 6., 8.])]`). The `envs` field (string name of the geometry) is read too, but only to record the environment name in `metadata['session_info']`; it does not enter `input`.\n\nii.\n```python\n        envs = [e[0] for e in dat['envs']]\n        blocked = dat['blocked']\n        ...\n            geom = geometry_vector(blocked[day])\n```\n\niii. The README defines `blocked` as the \"location of blocked (occluded) partitions in 3x3 design of environment ... organized in the following way – [[0, 1, 2], [3, 4, 5], [6, 7, 8]]. If no partitions are blocked, value is -1.\" The AI noted that the paper's own code never uses `blocked` (it rebuilds geometry from the env name via `get_env_mat`): \"Code doesn't use 'blocked'; env geometry comes from get_env_mat by env name\", and so it verified the indexing convention of `blocked` itself against behavioural occupancy (step 16–17) before using it — finding that blocked flat index = ybin*3 + xbin with xbin = pos[0]//25, ybin = pos[1]//25, and confirming after conversion that only 3e-5 of timepoints fall in a bin flagged blocked.\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Environment geometry*?\n\ni. `blocked[day]` is unwrapped (`[0]`, then `ravel`/`atleast_1d`) into a flat array of indices and converted to a 9-dimensional binary (float32) vector: entry *i* = 1 if partition *i* is blocked, 0 otherwise; negative entries (the `-1` \"nothing blocked\" sentinel) are skipped, giving the all-zero vector for open-square sessions. The vector is static: the *same* 9-vector (a copy per trial) is used for every trial of the session, stored as shape (9,) per trial. Input names are `blocked_x{i%3}_y{i//3}`, matching the `ybin*3+xbin` convention.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef geometry_vector(blocked_day):\n    \"\"\"9-dim binary vector, 1 if that partition of the 3x3 grid is blocked.\"\"\"\n    vec = np.zeros(N_SPATIAL_BINS * N_SPATIAL_BINS, dtype=np.float32)\n    b = np.atleast_1d(np.array(blocked_day[0]).ravel())\n    for i in b:\n        if i >= 0:\n            vec[int(i)] = 1.0\n    return vec\n...\n            geom = geometry_vector(blocked[day])\n            ...\n                input_trials.append(geom.copy())\n...\n        'input_names': [f'blocked_x{ i % N_SPATIAL_BINS }_y{ i // N_SPATIAL_BINS }'\n                        for i in range(N_SPATIAL_BINS * N_SPATIAL_BINS)],\n        ...\n            'input_convention': '1 = partition blocked (inaccessible), 0 = accessible',\n```\n\niii. The instructions specify \"Environment geometry, representing which parts of the arena are blocked. Static per-trial\", and the format allows a static `(d_input,)` vector per trial. A 9-dim indicator over the same 3 × 3 partition grid used for the output is the most direct encoding of the 10 geometries and lets the decoder relate geometry to position bins element-wise. The AI's post-conversion check (position essentially never falls in a blocked partition) validated both the encoding and the index convention.\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Mouse position* derived from?\n\ni. From the `position` field, `position[day]` of shape (2, n_frames): x and y head-tracking coordinates in cm, already in the range 0–75 with no NaNs (verified by the AI before writing the script).\n\nii.\n```python\n        position = dat['position']  # (n_days, 2, n_frames), cm\n        ...\n            pos = bin_time(position[day].astype(np.float64), TEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES)\n```\n\niii. The README describes `position` as \"x-y position data for all days ... x-y position in first dimension, and number of temporal bins / frames in second dimension\", and the methods state position came from DeepLabCut head tracking recorded on the same 30 Hz DAQ stream as the imaging. The AI checked the ranges (\"Position in cm 0-75\") and the NaN fraction (0) in its exploration step.\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Mouse position*?\n\ni. The (2, n_frames) position is cast to float64 and averaged over the same non-overlapping 3-frame blocks used for the neural data, yielding (2, n_bins) at 100 ms. It is then discretized (4-c). No smoothing, interpolation, speed filtering or per-session rescaling is applied; the arena extent is taken as the known physical 75 cm rather than estimated from the data.\n\nii.\n```python\n            # position: average within the same time bins, then spatially discretize\n            pos = bin_time(position[day].astype(np.float64), TEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES)\n            bin_size_cm = ARENA_SIZE / N_SPATIAL_BINS\n            xb = np.clip((pos[0] // bin_size_cm).astype(int), 0, N_SPATIAL_BINS - 1)\n            yb = np.clip((pos[1] // bin_size_cm).astype(int), 0, N_SPATIAL_BINS - 1)\n            pos_bin = (yb * N_SPATIAL_BINS + xb).astype(np.int64)\n```\n\niii. This mirrors `utils.fit_decoder`, which average-pools the behavioural stream with the same `AvgPool1d(kernel_size=temporal_bin_size)` used for the traces and only then takes integer bins — so position and neural activity always describe the same 100 ms window. Using the physical arena size (75 cm, from the methods) rather than the paper's per-session `nanmax` normalisation is the safer choice here because in deformed geometries the animal cannot reach all walls, so per-session maxima would shift bin edges between sessions and break comparability with the fixed `blocked` indices.\n\n## 4-c. How is `output` *Mouse position* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Into the 3 × 3 = 9 spatial bins of 25 cm required by the instructions, which coincide with the experiment's 3 × 3 partition design. x and y are floor-divided by 25 cm and clipped to [0, 2] (the clip catches the boundary value x = 75.0 exactly), and the flat class label is `ybin*3 + xbin`, i.e. the same convention as `blocked`. The result is a single time-varying integer output of shape (1, 600) per trial, with `output_names = ['position_bin']` and `output_values = ['x0_y0', 'x1_y0', ..., 'x2_y2']` ordered to match.\n\nii.\n```python\n            xb = np.clip((pos[0] // bin_size_cm).astype(int), 0, N_SPATIAL_BINS - 1)\n            yb = np.clip((pos[1] // bin_size_cm).astype(int), 0, N_SPATIAL_BINS - 1)\n            pos_bin = (yb * N_SPATIAL_BINS + xb).astype(np.int64)\n...\n    output_values = []\n    for idx in range(N_SPATIAL_BINS * N_SPATIAL_BINS):\n        x = idx % N_SPATIAL_BINS\n        y = idx // N_SPATIAL_BINS\n        output_values.append(f'x{x}_y{y}')\n...\n            'position_bin_convention': 'bin index = ybin*3 + xbin, same as the blocked field',\n```\n\niii. The Decoder Task specifies \"Mouse position discretized into 3 x 3 = 9 spatial bins\", and the paper itself partitions \"an open square (75 × 75 cm) into a 3 × 3 grid space\". The AI did not assume the flattening convention: it compared per-bin occupancy against the `blocked` indices for several environments (`t`, `rectangle`, `l`) and concluded \"blocked flat index = ybin*3 + xbin where xbin=pos[0]//25, ybin=pos[1]//25 (verified ...)\", then re-verified on the finished pickle that the animal is in a blocked bin for only ~3e-5 of timepoints.\n\n## 4-d. How is `output` *Mouse position* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Both streams are acquired simultaneously at 30 Hz by the same DAQ and are already frame-aligned in the file, so alignment reduces to applying identical temporal binning and identical trial slicing to both. The AI additionally truncates to the common length `n_bins = min(neural.shape[1], pos_bin.shape[0])` before cutting trials, guarding against any length mismatch between the two streams.\n\nii.\n```python\n            neural = bin_time(tr, TEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES).astype(np.float32)\n            pos = bin_time(position[day].astype(np.float64), TEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES)\n            ...\n            n_bins = min(neural.shape[1], pos_bin.shape[0])\n            n_trials = n_bins // BINS_PER_TRIAL\n            ...\n            for t in range(n_trials):\n                sl = slice(t * BINS_PER_TRIAL, (t + 1) * BINS_PER_TRIAL)\n                neural_trials.append(np.ascontiguousarray(neural[:, sl]))\n                output_trials.append(pos_bin[sl][None, :].copy())\n```\n\niii. The methods state \"The DAQ simultaneously acquired behavioral and cellular imaging streams at 30 Hz ... and all recorded frames were timestamped for post-hoc alignment\", and both arrays in the distributed data have the same frame count, so no resampling or timestamp interpolation is needed. Using the same `sl` for both streams, and the same 3-frame pooling, reproduces `fit_decoder`'s alignment exactly.\n\n## 5. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n\ni. Missing-data handling: (a) cells not registered on a day are NaN — these are dropped per session rather than zero-filled (`~np.isnan(tr).any(axis=1)`), and `np.nansum` is used when counting events so NaN cells cannot corrupt the activity criterion; (b) near-silent cells (≤5 events) are dropped; (c) position has no missing values in this dataset (the AI checked the NaN fraction = 0), so no interpolation is implemented — position NaNs, if they existed, would propagate silently through `//` and `astype(int)`; (d) position values exactly at the arena edge (75.0 cm) would fall in bin 3 and are pulled back by `np.clip`; (e) a possible neural/behaviour length mismatch is absorbed by truncating to `min(...)`; (f) leftover frames at the end of a session (not a whole 3-frame bin, then not a whole 600-bin trial) are silently discarded — at most ~0.1% of each session; (g) sessions too short to give 2 trials are skipped with a printed message.\n\nii.\n```python\n            registered = ~np.isnan(tr).any(axis=1)\n            events = np.nansum(tr, axis=1)\n            keep = registered & (events > EVENT_THRESHOLD)\n...\n            xb = np.clip((pos[0] // bin_size_cm).astype(int), 0, N_SPATIAL_BINS - 1)\n            yb = np.clip((pos[1] // bin_size_cm).astype(int), 0, N_SPATIAL_BINS - 1)\n...\n            n_bins = min(neural.shape[1], pos_bin.shape[0])\n            n_trials = n_bins // BINS_PER_TRIAL\n            if n_trials < 2:\n                print(f'  skipping {animal} day {day}: too short')\n                continue\n```\n\niii. The README states unregistered cells \"will appear as nan the same shape\", so dropping (not imputing) them is the documented interpretation; the AI verified that the resulting registered-cell count reproduces the paper's 69,744 rate maps, which is strong evidence the NaN criterion is the right one. Clipping and the `min()` truncation are defensive one-liners that cost nothing; discarding the sub-minute remainder is forced by the requirement that all trials have equal length.\n\n## 6-a. What are the most time-consuming steps of the code?\n\ni. The dominant costs are I/O and dense arithmetic on full-session arrays: (1) `joblib.load` of each animal file (70–150 MB compressed expanding to a (n_days, n_cells, n_frames) float64 `trace`, ~9 GB for a 31-day/515-cell animal, plus the `SFPs`/`maps` fields that are loaded but never used); (2) `gaussian_filter1d` over the (n_kept_cells, ~72,000) float32 trace of every one of the 207 sessions — the single largest compute item; (3) the `bin_time` reshape-and-mean, which materialises another full-size array; (4) `pickle.dump` of the 6.2 GB result and, downstream, its re-loading by the decoder. The run took several minutes of wall time, essentially all in (1)–(2). The AI never profiled these steps; it only monitored progress while the conversion ran.\n\nii.\n```python\n        dat = joblib.load(os.path.join(DATA_DIR, animal))[animal]\n...\n            tr = gaussian_filter1d(tr, sigma=SMOOTH_SIGMA, axis=1)\n            neural = bin_time(tr, TEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES).astype(np.float32)\n...\n    with open(OUT_FILE, 'wb') as f:\n        pickle.dump(data, f, protocol=4)\n```\n\niii. No explicit justification is given in the trajectory. The AI was aware of the scale (\"Data is large: ~69,744 cell-sessions × 72,000 frames; time bin size choice matters for memory\", \"dataset ~6.5GB at 100ms bins\") and chose the 100 ms binning partly for that reason, and it deletes each animal's arrays after use (`del dat, trace, position`) to keep peak memory bounded.\n\n## 6-b. What loops in the code could have been vectorized to improve efficiency?\n\ni. The code is already vectorized in the expensive places (smoothing, pooling and discretization are whole-array numpy operations). The remaining loops are (1) the animal and day loops, which are inherent to the per-session structure (each day has a different cell set and different geometry, so they cannot be merged); (2) the inner trial loop, which does 39 slice-and-copy operations per session — this could be replaced by a single reshape/`np.split` into (n_trials, n_cells, 600) views instead of 39 `ascontiguousarray` copies; (3) the `for i in b: vec[int(i)] = 1.0` loop in `geometry_vector`, which is a 1–4-element loop that could be a single fancy-index assignment `vec[b[b >= 0].astype(int)] = 1.0`. All of these are negligible relative to the per-session filtering and smoothing.\n\nii.\n```python\n            for t in range(n_trials):\n                sl = slice(t * BINS_PER_TRIAL, (t + 1) * BINS_PER_TRIAL)\n                neural_trials.append(np.ascontiguousarray(neural[:, sl]))\n                input_trials.append(geom.copy())\n                output_trials.append(pos_bin[sl][None, :].copy())\n...\n    b = np.atleast_1d(np.array(blocked_day[0]).ravel())\n    for i in b:\n        if i >= 0:\n            vec[int(i)] = 1.0\n```\n\niii. Not discussed in the trajectory. The per-trial copies are arguably intentional rather than wasteful: the target format is a list of per-trial arrays, and copying releases the reference to the whole-session array so it can be freed (though it also doubles peak memory for the session while both exist).\n\n## 6-c. What processing does the code repeat multiple times?\n\ni. Little is genuinely recomputed. The repeated work is: (1) the static 9-dim geometry vector is `.copy()`-ed once per trial — 39 identical 9-element arrays per session (8,187 copies overall), where one shared array per session would do (this is duplication of storage rather than of computation, and keeps trials independent); (2) `bin_time` is invoked separately for the neural and the behavioural stream, which is unavoidable since only the traces are smoothed first; (3) NaN inspection of the trace is done twice over the same array — once by `np.isnan(tr).any(axis=1)` and once implicitly by `np.nansum(tr, axis=1)`; (4) per-session bookkeeping such as `registered.sum()` is evaluated twice in adjacent lines. (2)–(4) are single passes over data already in cache and are immaterial.\n\nii.\n```python\n            registered = ~np.isnan(tr).any(axis=1)\n            events = np.nansum(tr, axis=1)\n            keep = registered & (events > EVENT_THRESHOLD)\n            n_cells_total += registered.sum()\n            n_dropped_silent += int(registered.sum() - keep.sum())\n...\n                input_trials.append(geom.copy())\n```\n\niii. Not discussed in the trajectory. The per-trial `geom.copy()` matches the required output structure (one entry per trial) and avoids aliasing between trials.\n\n## 6-d. What unnecessary processing does the code do that is discarded in downstream analyses?\n\ni. (1) `joblib.load` pulls the whole animal dict into memory, including `SFPs` (35×35×n_cells×n_days), `centroids` and the three `maps` arrays, none of which are used — a large amount of I/O and RAM for nothing; the code cannot avoid this with joblib without touching the loader, but it also never `del`s the dict until the end of the animal loop. (2) Smoothing and pooling are applied to the *entire* session including the tail frames that are then discarded when the session is cut into whole trials (~0.1%). (3) `envs` is parsed for every animal but only ends up as a descriptive string in `session_info` — harmless, and useful provenance. (4) `n_cells_total`/`n_dropped_silent` are accumulated purely for a diagnostic print. (5) The biggest downstream cost is not computation but the representation choice: smoothing turns an extremely sparse binary matrix into a dense float32 one, yielding a 6.2 GB pickle that the decoder must load and PCA in full; storing the unsmoothed binary trace (or a sparse representation) and smoothing inside the decoder would carry the same information far more cheaply. (6) Nothing else produced by the script is dropped by `train_decoder.py` — every field it writes is either required by the format or read by `verify_data_format`.\n\nii.\n```python\n        dat = joblib.load(os.path.join(DATA_DIR, animal))[animal]   # also loads SFPs, centroids, maps\n        ...\n            tr = gaussian_filter1d(tr, sigma=SMOOTH_SIGMA, axis=1)   # applied to frames later discarded\n        ...\n        del dat, trace, position\n...\n    print(f'Sessions: {len(neural_all)}, cells (registered): {n_cells_total}, '\n          f'dropped near-silent: {n_dropped_silent}')\n```\n\niii. Not discussed as waste in the trajectory; the AI's only related reasoning is that it weighed the output size when choosing the 100 ms bin (\"dataset ~6.5GB at 100ms bins\") and accepted it, and that it verified the converted file end-to-end (format check, decoder training to 0.612 validation balanced accuracy vs 0.111 chance) rather than trimming it further.\n","structuredPatch":[],"originalFile":null,"userModified":false}}

Only neurons with at least one valid value are kept.\n\nii.\n```python\nactive_mask = ~np.all(np.isnan(trace), axis=0)\ntrace = trace[:, active_mask].astype(np.float32).T\n```\n\niii. The `trace` array contains NaN columns for neurons not recorded in a given session (the same animal may have different neurons active across sessions). The 2D position is discretized into a 3×3 grid (9 classes). Each axis is divided into 3 equal bins spanning the 75 cm arena. The grid label is computed as `y_bin * 3 + x_bin`.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef discretize_position(position, n_grid=N_GRID, arena_size=ARENA_SIZE):\n    edges = np.linspace(0, arena_size, n_grid + 1)[1:-1]\n    x_bin = np.clip(np.digitize(position[0], edges), 0, n_grid - 1)\n    y_bin = np.clip(np.digitize(position[1], edges), 0, n_grid - 1)\n    return (y_bin * n_grid + x_bin).astype(np.int8)\n```\n\niii. A 3×3 grid gives 9 position classes, providing a coarse but balanced spatial discretization. `np.clip` ensures positions at the arena boundaries are assigned to valid bins.\n\n## 4-d. trials.\n\n    The last chunk is dropped if shorter than trial_length.\n\n    Returns:\n        list of arrays, each of shape (d, trial_length) or (trial_length,)\n    \"\"\"\n    if data.ndim == 1:\n        n_trials = len(data) // trial_length\n        return [data[i * trial_length:(i + 1) * trial_length] for i in range(n_trials)]\n    else:\n        n_trials = data.shape[1] // trial_length\n        return [data[:, i * trial_length:(i + 1) * trial_length] for i in range(n_trials)]\n\n\ndef process_mat_file(filepath):\n    \"\"\"Process a single .mat file.\n\n    Each recording session in the file becomes a separate session.\n    Each session is split into 1-minute trials.\n    Only neurons actually recorded are kept.\n\n    Returns:\n        sessions: list of dicts with keys 'neural', trace_refs.shape[0]\n\n    sessions = []\n\n    for i in range(n_recording_sessions):\n        # trace: (timepoints, neurons) -> keep only recorded neurons -> (n_active, n_timepoints)\n        trace = f[trace_refs[i][0]][:]  # (timepoints, neurons)\n        active_mask = ~np.all(np.isnan(trace), axis=0)\n        trace = trace[:, active_mask].astype(np.float32).T  # (n_active, n_timepoints)\n\n        # position -> discretized class labels (1, n_timepoints)\n        position = f[pos_refs[i][0]][:].T  # (2, n_timepoints)\n        output = discretize_position(position)[np.newaxis, :]  # (1, n_timepoints)\n\n        # blocked -> one-hot (n_blocked_positions,)\n        blk_indices = f[blk_refs[i]][:].flatten()\n        blocked = encode_blocked(blk_indices)\n\n        # split into 1-minute trials\n        neural_trials = split_into_trials(trace)\n        output_trials = split_into_trials(output)\n        input_trials = [blocked] * len(neural_trials)\n\n        n_active = int(active_mask.sum())\n        sessions.append({\n            'neural': neural_trials,\n            'input': input_trials,\n            'output': output_trials,\n            'n_neurons': n_active,\n        })\n\n    f.close()\n    return sessions\n\n\ndef main():\n    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(description='Convert lee2025 .mat files to pickle format')\n    parser.add_argument('output', f\"output classes={output_classes.astype(int)}, \"\n                  f\"blocked={blocked_str}\")\n\n    n_classes = N_GRID ** 2\n    output_values = [[f\"grid_{i}\" for i in range(n_classes)]]\n\n # frames, utils.fit_decoder gaussian_filter1d sigma\nTRIAL_SECONDS = 60.0       # 1-minute trials\nARENA_SIZE = 75.0          # cm, full square environment\nN_SPATIAL_BINS = 3         # 3 x 3 TEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES))\n\n\ndef bin_time(x, n_frames_bin):\n    \"\"\"Average non-overlapping bins of n_frames_bin along the last axis.\"\"\"\n    n = x.shape[-1] // n_frames_bin\n    x = x[..., :n * n_frames_bin]\n    return x.reshape(*x.shape[:-1], n, n_frames_bin).mean(axis=-1)\n\n\ndef geometry_vector(blocked_day):\n    \"\"\"9-dim binary vector, 1 if that partition of the 3x3 grid is blocked.\"\"\"\n    vec = np.zeros(N_SPATIAL_BINS * N_SPATIAL_BINS, dtype=np.float32)\n    b = np.atleast_1d(np.array(blocked_day[0]).ravel())\n    for i in b:\n        if i >= 0:\n blocked = dat['blocked']\n        n_days = trace.shape[0]\n\n        for day in range(n_days):\n            tr = trace[day]\n            # keep only cells registered on this day (unregistered cells analysis\n            events = np.nansum(tr, axis=1)\n            keep = registered & (events > EVENT_THRESHOLD)\n            n_cells_total += registered.sum()\n            n_dropped_silent += They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n\n### Consistency requirements\n\nYour processing and formatting must **match the reference paper and code** with respect to:\n- Loading of data\n- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n\nDiscrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above, or the task of training a neural decoder. During testing, a reference copy of `/app/train_decoder.py` will be run on your converted data. Data statistics and decoder accuracy will be compared to those achieved by human-written conversion code.\n\n## Success Criteria\n\nA successful conversion should:\n1. All required files must be created:\n  - `/app/convert_data.py`\n  - `/app/converted_data.pkl`\n2. Match the target data structure exactly\n3. Have consistent dimensions across trials/sessions\n4. Include all relevant data from the source\n5. Match information provided in the reference texts, code, and these instructions\n6. Outputs must match expert-written conversion code in:\n  - Dataset size and distribution statistics\n  - Decoder accuracy\n7. Each decision about how to load, filter, process, align, and save the data will be compared with the decision made by an expert. Decisions do not need to match the expert's: a different decision that is equally well justified is acceptable, while poorly justified decisions will be flagged as concerning or incorrect. Your code must implement the decisions as you describe them.\n\n\nCurrent terminal state:\nCurrent Terminal Screen:\nroot@f54581a2b251:/app#\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\n\nREASON: \nTOOLS: []\nOBS: \n\nSTEP 2 [agent]\nMSG: Analysis: Starting fresh. We recorded large neural populat\r\nions in CA1 with miniscope calcium imaging while mice freely explored a sequence of 10 geometrically distinct environments (Figures 1A, 1B, and S1). Importantly\r\n, we selected these distinct geometries to induce strong remapping within the same environment across the entire CA1 population. Following habituation to the sq\r\nuare arena, animals were exposed to a random sequence of geometries across days that started and ended with the square. The same sequence was repeated up to thr\r\nee times within animals but was randomized across mice (Figures 1C and 1D).\r\n\r\nMethods\r\n\r\nIn vivo calcium videos were recorded with a UCLA miniscope (v3; miniscope.org) containing a monochrome CMOS imaging sensor (MT9V032C12STM, ON Semiconductor) con\r\nnected to a custom data acquisition (DAQ) box (miniscope.org) with a lightweight, flexible coaxial cable. The DAQ was connected to a PC with a USB 3.0 SuperSpee\r\nd cable and controlled with Miniscope custom acquisition software (miniscope.org\r\n). The outgoing excitation LED was set to between 2-8% (∼0.05-0.2 mW), depending on the mouse to maximize signal quality with the minimum possible excitation li\r\nght to mitigate the risk of photobleaching. Gain was adjusted to match the dynamic range of the recorded video to the fluctuations of the calcium signal for eac\r\nh recording to avoid saturation. Behavioral video data were recorded by a webcam mounted above the environment. The DAQ simultaneously acquired behavioral and c\r\nellular imaging streams at 30 Hz as uncompressed AVI files and all recorded frames were timestamped for post-hoc alignment.\r\nAll recording environments were constructed of white Lego base plates (Lego, Inc) and white painted plywood walls. The full square environment was 75 cm x 75 cm\r\n. The external walls were 60 cm tall, while inserted partition walls were 25 cm tall. A blue Lego baseplate was affixed at the top-center of the Eastern wall to\r\n serve as an orienting visual feature. During recording, the environment was dimly lit. All sessions were 40 min, and one session was recorded per day to avoid\r\nphotobleaching. The order of the environmental configurations was randomized for each mouse, but repeated across sequences such that repeated configurations wer\r\ne always equidistant in time.\r\n\r\nData preprocessing\r\n\r\nCalcium imaging data were preprocessed prior to analyses via a pipeline of open-source MATLAB (MathWorks; version R2015a) functions to correct for motion artifa\r\ncts,47 segment cells and extract transients.45,48 To extract the rising phase of transients from each filtered calcium trace, we proceeded as follows. First, we\r\n computed the derivative of the calcium signal, smoothed with a gaussian kernel with a standard deviation of 5 frames. Next, because calcium transients around t\r\nhe baseline can only be positive, we estimated the variance in the derivative of the smoothed calcium signal on the basis of noise via a half-normal distributio\r\nn such that:\r\n\r\n<math><mrow is=\"true\"><mi is=\"true\">N</mi><mi is=\"true\">O</mi><mi is=\"true\">I</mi><mi is=\"true\">S</mi><mi is=\"true\">E</mi><mo linebreak=\"badbreak\" is=\"true\">=</\r\nmo><mfrac is=\"true\"><mrow is=\"true\"><mi is=\"true\">s</mi><mi is=\"true\">t</mi><mi is=\"true\">d</mi><mrow is=\"true\"><mo is=\"true\">(</mo><mrow is=\"true\"><mo is=\"true\r\n\">Δ</mo><mi is=\"true\">t</mi><mrow is=\"true\"><mo is=\"true\">(</mo><mrow is=\"true\"><mi is=\"true\">t</mi><mo linebreak=\"badbreak\" is=\"true\">&lt;</mo><mn is=\"true\">0<\r\n/mn></mrow><mo is=\"true\">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo is=\"true\">)</mo></mrow></mrow><msqrt is=\"true\"><mrow is=\"true\"><mn is=\"true\">1</mn><mo linebreak=\"badbreak\" is=\r\n\"true\">−</mo><mfrac is=\"true\"><mn is=\"true\">2</mn><mi is=\"true\">π</mi></mfrac></mrow></msqrt></mfrac></mrow></math>\r\n\r\nWhere is the smoothed time-derivative of the median-subtracted calcium trace . you can 1) open terminal or command prompt, 2) navigate to the desired working directory, and 3) run the following to clone the reposito\r\nry:\r\n```\r\n$ git clone https://github.com/jquinnlee/georepca1.git\r\n```\r\n\r\nTo run code with all dependencies installed you can **create virtual environment from the environment.yml file** in the command line:\r\n```\r\n$ conda env create --name georepca1 --file environment.yml\r\n```\r\n\r\nFinally, in Python add the src folder (containing all custom functions and main.py) to your path:\r\n```\r\n$ import sys\r\n$ sys.path.append(\"your_working_directory_name/georepca1/src\")\r\n```\r\n\r\nDataset can be freely downloaded from **Zenodo** (https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.13993254) and **manually added to the \"georepca1/data\" folder**.\r\n\r\nTo run all main analyses and generate figures, you can **either run main.py** after defining your local path to the georepca1 folder, or follow through **Jupyte\r\nr notebooks** located in the \"georepca1/demos\" folder.\r\n*Note that some results build across notebooks, and thus each should be run to completion before moving onto the next.*\r\n\r\n___________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________\r\n# Data organization\r\n\r\nThe dataset (Python joblib files or MATLAB .mat files) are given names of animal IDs from the original study that can be downloaded from Zenodo and contain the\r\nfollowing fields in each file:\r\n\r\n**SFPs**: spatial footprints (also known as ROI) for every registered cell, centered over each cell. Shape - Dimx, dimy, number of SFPs (ROIs), number of days.\r\nIf cell is not registered it will be nan along dimx and dimy for a given day.\r\n\r\n**blocked**: location of blocked (occluded) partitions in 3x3 design of environment. Location of partitions are shown in paper, but are organized in the followi\r\nng way – [[0, 1, 2], [3, 4, 5], [6, 7, 8]]. If no partitions are blocked, value is -1.\r\n\r\n**centroids**: centroid of spatial footprint. Shape – number of cells, x-y location, number of days.\r\n\r\n**envs**: environment shape identified with string name\r\n\r\n**maps**: three types of maps generated from the dataset. “sampling” is the occupancy of animal in each spatial bin, shape – xbins, ybins, number of days. “smoo\r\nthed” is the event rate map smoothed with 2.5 cm gaussian kernel, shape – xbins, ybins, number of cells, number of days. “unsmoothed” is the same event rate map\r\n data without smoothing.\r\n\r\n**position**: x-y position data for all days. List shape number of days, with shape on each day indicating x-y position in first dimension, and number of tempor\r\nal bins / frames in second dimension.\r\n\r\n**trace**: rise-extracted calcium traces, where “1” indicates a significant event. See paper for details on processing pipeline. If cell is not registered on gi\r\nven day, will appear as nan the same shape.\r\n\r\n___________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________\r\n# Cite\r\n**Please cite our original paper by Lee et al. Similar results are observed when the likelihood of spiking was inferred via a second-order autoregressive deconvolution instead of transient r\r\nising-phase extraction. All statistical comparisons were performed with Scipy and Statsmodels libraries in Python 3.8.\r\n\r\nPlace cell identification\r\n\r\nTo detect place cells, we generated rate maps for each session half (first and second 20 min) and compared the resulting Pearson correlation to the maps generat\r\ned from 1000 circular shuffles or the corresponding position data. Place cells were identified as those whose split-half rate map correlation exceeded the 99th\r\npercentile of the corresponding shuffled distribution for each cell.\r\n\r\nBayesian decoding of animal position\r\n\r\nTo decode animals’ position from calcium traces within each session, we performed a 5-fold split of spatially binned position and trace data and transformed the\r\n binned positions to a one-hot vector.  Decoding error was then estimated as the Euclidean distance between the predicted and actual spatial bin position of the\r\n animal on withheld data.\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nroot@f54581a2b251:/app# grep -n Options are: \"square\", \"o\", \"t\", \"u\", \"rectangle\", \"+\", \"i\", \"l\",\r\n    \"bit donut\", or \"glenn\"\r\n    :return: binary 3x3 matrix of the environment\r\n    '''\r\n    if env == environment using animal behavior. Matrix will contain\r\n    transition probabilities for each pair of states (bins) in the environment.\r\n    :param behav: x-y position of animal in environment from dataset\r\n    :param maps: rate maps used for rounding discrete behavioral states\r\n    :param step_size: step in number of frames recorded at 30Hz recording to iterate across (e.g., every 15 steps = 2Hz)\r\n    :param n_bins: number of spatial bins in x and y dimensions of the environment\r\n    :param buffer: buffer size used for rounding discrete behavioral states\r\n    :return: transition matrix as probabilities, and total counts of transitions between states\r\n    \"\"\"\r\n    # make a deepcopy of behav and trace data as to not change original inputs\r\n    behav = deepcopy(behav)\r\n    # bin down behavioural data\r\n    behav_max = behav.max(axis=0).max(axis=1)\r\n    bin_down = (behav_max.max() + buffer) / n_bins\r\n    behav /= bin_down\r\n    # grab the number of days to iterate across, and initialize some variables to return and use as function params\r\n    n_days = behav.shape[2]\r\n    # create template maps for all days to round x-y position from behaviour\r\n    temp_maps = np.nansum(maps, axis=2).astype(bool)\r\n    # initialize transition matrix\r\n    transition_mat = np.zeros([temp_maps.shape[0] * temp_maps.shape[1],\r\n                               temp_maps.shape[0] * temp_maps.shape[1], n_days]).astype(float)\r\n    transition_mat_counts = np.zeros_like(transition_mat)\r\n    t1_map, t2_map = np.zeros([n_bins, n_bins]), np.zeros([n_bins, n_bins])\r\n    for d in tqdm(range(n_days), desc=f'Calculating transition matrices across {n_days} days', leave=True, position=0):\r\n        target_behav = np.floor(behav[::step_size, :, d]).astype(int)\r\n        for i, (x, y) in enumerate(target_behav[:-1]):\r\n            t1_map[x, y] += 1\r\n            t2_map[target_behav[i+1].astype(int)[0], target_behav[i+1].astype(int)[1]] += 1\r\n            transition_mat[np.argwhere(t1_map.ravel())[0][0], np.argwhere(t2_map.ravel())[0][0], d] += 1\r\n            t1_map, t2_map = t1_map * 0, t2_map * 0\r\n        # convert into probabilities that sum to one across column axis\r\n        transition_mat_counts[:, :, d] = deepcopy(transition_mat[:, :, d])\r\n        transition_mat[:, :, d] = (transition_mat[:, :, d].T / transition_mat[:, :, d].T.sum(axis=0)).T\r\n    # return transition probabilities and count number\r\n    return np.nan_to_num(transition_mat), transition_mat_counts\r\n\r\n\r\n########################################################################################################################\r\n# Rate map generation and split-half reliability measures\r\ndef get_rate_maps(position, trace, n_bins=15, fps=30, buffer=1e-5, filter_size=1.5):\r\n    \"\"\"\r\n    Create rate maps from animal position and calcium trace data (single session).\r\n    :param position: x-y position of the animal, shape n frames (row) by n dimensions (2 columns)\r\n    :param trace: calcium trace data, shape n frames (row) by n cells\r\n    :param n_bins: number of spatial bins in x- and y-dimensions\r\n    :param fps: frames per second of recording\r\n    :param buffer: buffer size for rounding binned position data\r\nroot@f54581a2b251:/app#\r\n\n\nSTEP 7 [agent]\nMSG: Analysis: Seen get_env_mat. number) for smoothing event rate maps\r\n    :return: event rate maps, occupancy map (proportion of time in each bin), and average event rate for all cells\r\n    \"\"\"\r\n    # time is row axis for position and trace data\r\n    # First determine the length of the recording and number of neurons\r\n    len_recording = trace.shape[0]\r\n    n_cells = trace.shape[1]\r\n    # Initialize rate maps and count map (which will count the number of times each bin is visited)\r\n    rate_maps = np.zeros([n_cells, n_bins, n_bins])\r\n    count_map = np.zeros([n_bins, n_bins])\r\n    # Create a binned and add value of each trace\r\n    # to rate map and a one to the same position on the count map\r\n    for t, (x, y) in enumerate(position_binned):\r\n        rate_maps[:, x, y] += trace[t, :]\r\n    Calculate split-half reliability of event rate maps for circularly shuffled position and calcium traces.\r\n    :param position: x-y position of the animal, shape n frames (row) by n dimensions (2 columns)\r\n    :param trace: calcium trace data, shape n frames (row) by n cells\r\n    :param nsims: number of shuffles to perform\r\n    :param min_time: minimum time to circularly shift data in seconds\r\n    :param fps: frames per second of original data\r\n    :return: split-half correlation for shuffled data, shape n cells (rows) by nsims\r\n    \"\"\"\r\n    # get the length of the recording and number of cells\r\n    len_recording = trace.shape[0]\r\n    n_cells = trace.shape[1]\r\n    # initialize shuffle_corr\r\n    shuffle_corr = np.zeros([n_cells, nsims])\r\n    # reliability on shuffled data'):\r\n        shuffled_position = np.roll(position, np.random.randint(min_time * fps, len_recording - min_time * fps), axis=0)\r\n        shuffle_corr[:, i] = split-half correlation of event rate maps.\r\n    :param position: x-y position of the animal, shape n frames (row) by n dimensions (2 columns)\r\n    :param trace: calcium trace data, shape n frames (row) by n cells\r\n    :param nsims: number of shuffles to perform\r\n    :param alpha: p-value threshold to consider cell as place cell\r\n    :return: split-half reliability p-values for all cells, and place cells as boolean (n cells)\r\n    \"\"\"\r\n    n_cells = trace.shape[1]\r\n    # measure actual split-half\r\n    sh_actual = get_split_half(position, trace)\r\n    # measure shuffle split half\r\n    sh_shuffle = get_shuffle_split_half(position, trace, nsims)\r\n    # calculate p values\r\n    p_vals = np.zeros(n_cells)\r\n    place_cells = np.zeros(n_cells).astype(bool)\r\n    for cell in range(n_cells):\r\n        p_vals[cell] = 1 - ((sh_actual[cell] > sh_shuffle[cell, :]).sum() / nsims)\r\n        if p_vals[cell] < alpha:\r\n            if ~np.isnan(p_vals[cell]):\r\n                place_cells[cell] = True\r\n    p_vals[np.isnan(trace[0, :])] = np.nan\r\n    place_cells[np.isnan(trace[0, :])] = np.nan\r\n    return p_vals, place_cells\r\n\r\n\r\ndef get_shr_within(dat, animal, nsims=1000):\r\n    \"\"\"\r\n    Iterate through days of recordings for animal, return within-session split-half reliability p-values and place cells\r\n    :param dat: data dictionary for  :return: split-half reliability p-values and place cells as boolean (n cells).\r\n    \"\"\"\r\n    n_days = dat[animal]['trace'].shape[0]\r\n    place_cells = [None] * n_days\r\n    p_vals = [None] * n_days\r\n    # iterate through days\r\n    for day in range(n_days):\r\n        # get split-half reliability and place cells for each day\r\n        p_vals[day], place_cells[day] = get_place_cells(dat[animal]['position'][day].T,\r\n                                                        dat[animal]['trace'][day].T, nsims=nsims)\r\n    # convert to numpy array from list, and transpose\r\n    p_vals, place_cells = np.array(p_vals).T, np.array(place_cells).T\r\n    return p_vals, place_cells\r\nroot@f54581a2b251:/app#\r\n\n\nSTEP 8 [agent]\nMSG: Analysis: Need decoder \"\"\"\r\n    Decode animal temporal and spatially binned position with k-fold cross validation and naive Bayesian method.\r\n    :param behav: x-y position of animal shape n frames (row) by n Each holds one converted\r\n# session, so this also caps the extra memory at that many sessions.\r\n#\r\n# Every task asks for at least 16 CPUs. Throughput peaks where the thread count\r\n# matches the CPUs the container is allowed and falls away on both sides: measured\r\n# against one thread, 8 threads gave 5.7x, 16 gave 9.0x, 24 gave 7.0x and 32 gave\r\n# 5.8x. Past the limit the threads exhaust the cgroup's CPU budget early in each\r\n# period and are stopped until the next one, resuming with cold caches, which this\r\n# strided copy is especially sensitive to.\r\nCONVERSION_WORKERS = 16\r\n\r\n\r\nclass SessionData(torch.utils.data.Dataset):\r\n    \"\"\"Per-session tensors, converted on demand instead of all at once.\r\n\r\n    Indexed like the list this used to return: ``session_data[session]['neural']``.\r\n    Converts on every call, so bind it once per session:\r\n    ``batch = session_data[session]``.\r\n\r\n    Why: the previous version built every session's float32 tensors up front while\r\n    the caller's numpy arrays stayed alive -- two full copies of the dataset. The\r\n    source copy cannot be freed, because test_decoder_accuracy receives it from\r\n    pytest's module-scoped `submitted_data_full` fixture, which pins it for the whole\r\n    test module. So this second copy is the only one that can be avoided. On\r\n    mouseland/claude-code trial3 it took a 422 GB run to 984 GB and got it\r\n    OOM-killed on a 960 GB node.\r\n\r\n    Args:\r\n        neural: length nsessions; neural[session][trial] is (nneurons, T), any float\r\n            dtype.\r\n        input: length nsessions; input[session][trial] is (dinput,) for a per-trial\r\n            value or (dinput, T) for a time-varying one.\r\n        output: same convention as input, integer class labels.\r\n        dinput: input dimension, already inferred by the caller.\r\n        doutput: output dimension, already inferred by the caller.\r\n    \"\"\"\r\n\r\n    def __init__(self, neural: list, input: list, output: list,\r\n                 dinput: int, doutput: int):\r\n        self._neural, self._input, self._output = neural, input, output\r\n        self.dinput, self.doutput = dinput, doutput\r\n\r\n    def __len__(self) -> int:\r\n        return len(self._neural)\r\n\r\n    def n_nonempty(self) -> int:\r\n        \"\"\"Sessions holding at least one trial, counted without converting any.\"\"\"\r\n        return sum(1 for session in self._neural if len(session) > 0)\r\n\r\n    def iter_sessions(self, workers: int | None = None):\r\n        \"\"\"Yield (session, batch) for every session, converting several at a time.\r\n\r\n        Conversion transposes each trial into the destination, a strided copy that\r\n        leaves one core waiting on memory rather than saturating it, so several\r\n        sessions convert in less time than the sum of their parts. numpy releases\r\n        the GIL for the assignment, which is why threads suffice and no data has to\r\n        cross a process boundary.\r\n\r\n        At most `workers` conversions are in flight, so the extra memory is bounded\r\n        by that many sessions however long the dataset is.\r\n\r\n        Args:\r\n            workers: threads to convert on. None uses CONVERSION_WORKERS.\r\n\r\n        Yields:\r\n            (session, batch) in session order, batch as `__getitem__` returns it --\r\n            None for a session with no trials. Order and contents match a plain\r\n            loop over `self[session]`, so results do not depend on the thread count.\r\n        \"\"\"\r\n        if workers is None:\r\n            workers = CONVERSION_WORKERS\r\n        nsessions = len(self)\r\n        if workers <= 1:\r\n            for session in range(nsessions):\r\n                yield session, self[session]\r\n            return\r\n\r\n        with ThreadPoolExecutor(max_workers=workers) as pool:\r\n            # Submitted in order and popped in order, so the queue both bounds how\r\n            # many conversions run ahead and preserves the session sequence.\r\n            pending = deque()\r\n            submitted = 0\r\n            for session in range(nsessions):\r\n                while submitted < nsessions and len(pending) < workers:\r\n                    pending.append(pool.submit(self.__getitem__, submitted))\r\n                    submitted += 1\r\n                yield session, pending.popleft().result()\r\n\r\n    def __getitem__(self, session: int):\r\n        \"\"\"Build one session's tensors from its per-trial arrays.\r\n\r\n        Args:\r\n            session: index into the per-session lists.\r\n\r\n        Returns:\r\n            {'neural': float32 (rows, nneurons), 'input': float32 (rows, dinput),\r\n             'output': int64 (rows, doutput), 'nneurons': int}, where rows is the\r\n            session's total timepoints; or None when the session has no trials,\r\n            which is what the previous version stored for it.\r\n        \"\"\"\r\n        trials = self._neural[session]\r\n        if len(trials) == 0:\r\n            return None\r\n\r\n        rows = sum(trial.shape[1] for trial in trials)\r\n        nneurons = trials[0].shape[0]\r\n\r\n        # Preallocate and fill, rather than\r\n        # np.concatenate([trial.T.astype(np.float32) for trial in trials]): that\r\n        # holds a converted copy of every trial AND the concatenated result at the\r\n        # same moment. Assigning into the destination casts in place, so neither\r\n        # temporary is ever allocated. Per-trial inputs and outputs are broadcast\r\n        # into their row block instead of being np.tile'd first -- tile built a\r\n        # (d, T) array per trial only to transpose and copy it again.\r\n        neural_concat = np.empty((rows, nneurons), np.float32)\r\n        input_concat = np.empty((rows, self.dinput), np.float32)\r\n        output_concat = np.empty((rows, self.doutput), np.int64)\r\n\r\n        row = 0\r\n        for trial in range(len(trials)):\r\n            T = trials[trial].shape[1]\r\n            neural_concat[row:row + T] = trials[trial].T\r\n            input_trial = self._input[session][trial]\r\n            output_trial = self._output[session][trial]\r\n            # A 1-D per-trial value broadcasts across the block's T rows.\r\n            input_concat[row:row + T] = (input_trial.T if input_trial.ndim == 2\r\n                                         else input_trial)\r\n            output_concat[row:row + T] = (output_trial.T if output_trial.ndim == 2\r\n                                          else output_trial)\r\n            row += T\r\n\r\n        # from_numpy wraps these buffers rather than copying them.\r\n        return {\r\n            'neural': torch.from_numpy(neural_concat),\r\n            'input': torch.from_numpy(input_concat),\r\n            'output': torch.from_numpy(output_concat),\r\n            'nneurons': nneurons,\r\n        }\r\n\r\n\r\nroot@f54581a2b251:/app#\r\n\n\nSTEP 20 [agent]\nMSG: Analysis: Understood the data layout and binning conventions.
